Fortune | FORTUNE
Amazon workers on food stamps have tripled despite its record revenue—and it’s just the latest evidence of the new economy of shrinking labor shares
2026-09-13

Amazon workers on food stamps have tripled despite its record revenue—and it’s just the latest evidence of the new economy of shrinking labor shares

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
The U.S. workforce has just made the type of history it likely wishes it didn’t. Working Americans are taking home the smallest percentage of economic output, 52.8%, since the Bureau of Labor Statistics began tracking the metric in 1947. But as the share of wealth workers take home through wages—called labor share—is shrinking, corporate profits have exploded, with the S&P 500 index gaining 600% since the beginning of the century, while wages have increased just 12.5% over the same period, adjusted for inflation. In other words: Corporations are raking in cash, but American workers are reaping less and less of the rewards. The consequences associated with shrinking labor shares are now becoming tangible. A recent Government Accountability Office (GAO) report found that across the 11 states sampled, Amazon, the country’s largest company by revenue, has 12,346 workers on the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) and 11,338 relying on Medicaid. That was nearly triple the number of Amazon employees in need of federal assistance compared to 2020, when GAO conducted a similar report. During that same period, Amazon saw an increase in annual profits from $11.6 billion to $77.7 billion. Amazon’s 2025 revenue soared 12% year over year, from $638 billion to a record $717 billion. Amazon spokesperson Rachael Lighty told Fortune the conclusion drawn from the GAO report is “wrong,” and that it is misleading to look at raw numbers rather than percentages. “When you look at the actual facts, you see that Amazon pay is among the best in the industry, regular full-time employees have access to health care from their first day—at only $5 per week with $5 copays for employee-only coverage—and 74% of our regular full-time employees are enrolled in an Amazon health insurance plan, well above the 65% private-sector take-up rate for full-time workers,” she said in a statement. “We challenge other large retailers to provide the same Day 1 comprehensive health care benefits that we do.” Walmart and FedEx saw similar increases in the number of workers taking federal assistance, as did ride-share and delivery companies. Kathryn Larin, director for education, workforce, and income security issues at GAO, told Fortune the data illustrates that the Americans taking advantage of social safety net programs today are overwhelmingly in the workforce, with most working full-time. The income threshold for SNAP eligibility is about 130% of the poverty line, suggesting that despite many of these workers clocking in at their jobs, they still don’t have enough money to meet their basic needs. “What this analysis really points to is the large number of people who have very low incomes and continue to have very low income,” Larin said. “I mean, these are families that are really barely able to make ends meet, and yet they are working, and they are working a lot.” Diane Swonk, chief economist and managing director at KPMG, recently warned of the hidden consequences of a shrinking labor share, particularly that despite economic indicators suggesting the economy is stable, most Americans are facing an ongoing affordability crisis. KPMG found in February that since 1982, corporate profits as a share of U.S. GDP increased from 8% to 15.85%. During that same period, employee compensation as a share of U.S. GDP shrank from 66.6% to 61.9%. “This chart from my recent Economic Compass still haunts me,” Swonk said in a social media post at the time. “A friend refers to it as the ‘revolution chart,’ which [is] disturbing but telling. Inequality fuels social and economic instability.” This trend has been about 50 years in the making, according to Anna Stansbury, an assistant professor of work and organization studies at the MIT Sloan School of Management. Fewer workers in the U.S. are represented by unions—20.1% of U.S. workers in 1983 compared to 10.0% in 2025—giving them fewer opportunities to bargain for salaries and benefits, she noted. But more so, Stansbury blames the fissuring of the workplace, or the breakdown of the employer-employee relationship. In the past, the typical employer-employee relationship would be that of direct employment: A worker for a company does their work at the company they are employed by. For example, a large bank like Bank of America used to employ a janitor to clean its offices. But “in more and more cases, that’s not actually people’s experience of the workplace, particularly in lower middle-income jobs,” Stansbury told Fortune. Instead, large workplaces like retailers and banks hire gig workers or subcontractors to complete jobs once done by direct employees: Companies hire a security provider, which employs a security guard to work outside that large company’s office. Delivery drivers are contract workers, not full-time employees. As a result, companies don’t have to provide those workers equity or benefits. If a subcontractor violates labor law, the company contracting them is not liable. In the meantime, these large companies are not only saving money on benefits, but are also getting to argue that they are increasing efficiency by not spending resources on workers whose roles are not directly driving revenue, Stansbury explained. A bank should employ bankers, not janitors, to get the most bang for its buck, the thinking goes. Brent Neiman, a professor of economics at the University of Chicago, believes AI has been the key driver of diminishing labor shares. In a New York Times op-ed published this week, he argued the technology’s productivity promises pose the greatest threat to wages. “Anyone who has used ChatGPT can see how much work now done by people could soon be done by technology,” he wrote. Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok has already observed a correlation between AI adoption and lowered wages. In a white paper published in July, he and economist Sania Edlich found that among 321 occupations between 2015 and 2025, jobs with high exposure to AI saw a 6.7% decline in real wage growth after 2023 (following the wide release of ChatGPT), despite no employment changes, suggesting that while AI is not displacing jobs, it is associated with compressed wages. In other words, companies are seeing increases in productivity, but are using those increases as a cover to raise prices, while keeping wages stagnant. Stansbury has a slightly different theory about AI. While the technology could begin to have an aggregate impact on the labor market, it’s still too early to say whether today’s shrinking labor share is part of a broader economic cycle, or if it’s a secular event, she said. Unexpected inflation spikes, like what is currently happening, are usually associated with poorer real wages. If inflation is less volatile in the coming years and wage growth recovers, today’s trend of shrinking labor share could turn out to be cyclical, Stansbury said. On the other hand, Stansbury noted, a tightening labor market should increase labor share, and the labor market is already relatively tight. If inflation stabilizes, employment remains narrow, and the labor share increases, it will be a sign of an economic cycle completing itself. A bigger problem will be if wage growth stays low even if inflation improves and the labor market stays tight. “If those two things happen and the labor share continues to fall,” she said, “then it would suggest that there’s actually been a secular shift, a secular acceleration in the downward trend.” Sasha Rogelberg is a reporter and former editorial fellow on the news desk at Fortune, covering retail and the intersection of business and popular culture. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. How socialisation, skill and standing out can contribute to grad success
2026-09-14

How socialisation, skill and standing out can contribute to grad success

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Accenture’s Barry O’Keeffe, Laoise Macken and Stephen Crothers discuss what graduates can expect from a career that starts with a development programme. Graduate programmes are an ideal way for students and recent graduates to develop their skills, a network and also make plans for their futures. Taking the chance to come into a company at an early stage of one’s career and experience a range of departments, roles and responsibilities can expose new professionals to career routes they perhaps hadn’t considered. It can also cement in their minds the areas that they want to give particular focus to. Moreover, it can create a welcoming environment that prioritises easing new employees in, so that the experience, while focused on learning, upskilling and career growth, is not overwhelming. Speaking to SiliconRepublic.com, Barry O’Keeffe, a business and technology integration analyst at Accenture, explained that part of his role at the organisation involves working as an AI engineer for a large client. He said, “We’re building out a data warehouse to monitor operational metrics for trust and safety data – so content moderation. My priority at the moment is developing an agentic system, which automatically handles incoming bugs raised within the warehouse.” The socialisation and camaraderie that can grow within the Accenture graduate programme is, for O’Keeffe, among its greatest characteristics. “One of my favourite parts of Accenture is that when you join in your first week, you’re assigned a buddy, who’s a current graduate, and a people lead, who’s at manager level,” he said. “The buddy you can go to during your first few weeks in your first year with all of the questions that you will have. The people lead is there to help with your career progression. So, any guidance that you need throughout your time at Accenture.” The same experience is true for management consulting analyst Laoise Macken, who said, “I now live with two people from my start group back in September and that’s not an uncommon thing that you hear from the analysts in Accenture. People get really close really quick.” According to senior manager and delivery lead Stephen Crothers, this level of guidance is key to building up a graduate so that they have the skills needed for a long and successful career. “I think guidance and mentorship is huge in our graduate programme, but I equally think that’s really important throughout any kind of career,” he said. “I’ve been in Accenture for nearly nine years now and I would say that every different point in my career, whether it be a big milestone or a promotion, can always be attributed down to one of the many mentors that I’ve had throughout that stage of my career.” Of the qualities he would expect in a professional thinking of joining the graduate programme, he said: “The things that we look for are both on the skills and the behaviour side. However, skills can be taught – behaviours can’t. “Some of the key pieces that I look for whenever I’m doing my interviews are that curiosity. Another thing is around proactivity. We want someone that’s proactive, that doesn’t need to be given a kind of a defined roadmap of tasks to do, but to have that self-starter mentality.” Macken added, “A skill that I’ve found useful in my role here at Accenture is being adaptable. Things move really quickly here, and our clients are at the forefront of AI and leaders in their industry, which means that priorities can change quickly, and you need to be comfortable diving headfirst into the work around you. “So I think that people that are open-minded, hardworking and adaptable really thrive here. What surprised me most about the graduate programme is how strong the analyst community is.” Macken added: “Another skill that I’ve found really useful in my experience at Accenture has been communication. The teams that we work on are very cross-functional and range across a very wide level of seniority.” As a result, she finds that she is able to communicate confidently while also maintaining who she is as an individual and developing genuine relationships. She advised anyone considering a role as a graduate to have a ‘sponge-like’ mentality to soak up new skills and advice. “The people around you are absolute experts in their field and have such depth of industry knowledge that there’s not a single person that you’ll meet here that won’t be able to teach you something at all levels – analyst or MD. So yeah, forging genuine connections with people and asking questions and really trying to absorb as much as you can.” Be prepared for any interview, said Crothers, “then do more preparation” to really think about the questions you will be asked, the kind of answers you will give and how you express yourself. “That’s what helps you build out, I suppose, that skillset to take you to a place where, again, it would be unrealistic to not succeed.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. In a separate post, he wrote, “Boards are never boring,” with a ‘laughing-crying’ emoji accompanying the statement. Automattic is best known as the creator behind WordPress.com. It also owns the microblogging platform Tumblr, WooCommerce – a plugin for WordPress – and the online survey and quiz conductor CrowdSignal, formerly known as PollDaddy. Recovery readiness a missing link in cyber resilience, finds report
2026-09-14

Recovery readiness a missing link in cyber resilience, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Michelle Harris. Image: Joe Conroy Photography Triangle’s independent Cyber Recovery Readiness survey found that prolonged business outages are a major risk for organisations. According to new research based on Triangle Technology Services’ independent Cyber Recovery Readiness survey, organisations are facing significant challenges in preparing for and recovering from cyberattacks. The company surveyed 130 IT and security decision-makers to explore how ready companies are for a cyber threat, but also how they might navigate the aftermath. What was found is that prolonged business outages are a major risk factor for organisations, particularly as frontier AI accelerates cyber threats. Of those who participated, 64pc of companies were found to be missing a key risk remediation, 62pc confirmed that their organisation has isolated backups, but only 36pc could validate the integrity of that data when needed. Commenting on the results of the report, Michelle Harris, the director of cyber recovery at Triangle, said, “Many organisations are investing heavily in AI for prevention of cyber attacks, but far fewer regularly test their ability to recover from a breach. “Recovery readiness is now a critical business issue, and this survey provides valuable insight into how prepared organisations really are when disruption occurs.” Additional findings indicated that, of the participating organisations, 51pc have a clean recovery environment and 62pc have a dedicated, coordinated recovery team. Almost half of companies have tested or rehearsed their recovery in the last year. “While threat awareness is real and growing, it has not yet translated into the right kind of action,” said Harris. “The National Cyber Security Centre in Britain changed their focus from prevention to recovery.  They’re saying organisations should assume there will be an event.  That hasn’t filtered through to a lot of organisations, where they are still focusing on prevention, which they do still have to do, of course.” She added, “As cyberattacks become more sophisticated, businesses need to assume breaches will occur and ensure, through regular testing, they can recover operations quickly and securely. “Organisations that have implemented mature cyber recovery capabilities know in advance of a breach what data they will recover, where they will be recovering to and how long it will take, so there is no guesswork involved when everyone is under pressure.” In June, the Eir Business Hidden Cost of Cyber Risk report found that on average, cyberattacks are costing Irish small and medium-sized enterprises up to €3.4bn annually. It was discovered that the greatest impact is not from large-scale, one-off breaches, but frequent day-to-day cybersecurity-related disruptions that are driving losses for many Irish companies. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman at the World Economic Forum 2024. OpenAI is not going public this year, CEO Sam Altman told Fortune last Friday (11 September). “Right now would be an ill-advised moment to go public,” he told the publication, reasoning that AI safety should take priority over a blockbuster initial public offering. “We’ll do it when we’re ready.” “I would say not 2026 … We got a lot of stuff to do, like meeting this moment of what is going to be required for safety and alignment, and how the industry and governments can work together.” The ChatGPT creator filed to go public in June this year, but said that it could be a “while” before it ceases to be a private company. The Claude creator, he said, will use embedded third-party evaluators to verify AI safeguards moving forward. Altman also made similar commitments in a company-wide meeting, according to Bloomberg. Speaking to Fortune, the CEO said that OpenAI would be “incredibly profitable” even if the pace of development at the company is slow. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds
2026-09-13

IVCA raises alarm over foreign VC dominating Irish SME rounds

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-13

Visit Original Website
‘These levels are flashing red warning signals’, says IVCA chairperson Richard Watson. The Irish Venture Capital Association (IVCA) has raised concerns over the influence of international venture capital funding in Irish domestic companies, as foreign contributions represented 82pc of the total investment in Irish SMEs in the first half of 2026. “These levels are flashing red warning signals as they represent some of the highest on record and emphasise our over-exposure to overseas investors,” said IVCA chairperson Richard Watson of the VenturePulse survey data published today (13 September). Global geopolitical instability, including the war in Iran, as well as the significant concentration of US capital in blockbuster AI deals could well impact future international VC investment into Ireland, he said. Turbulent geopolitical conditions caused a 71pc fall-off in international investment in Q4 last year, leading to a 46pc decline in total funding for the quarter, while contributing to a 23pc drop-off in funding for the year. The “rollercoaster” year for funding was attributed to an “undoubted Trump effect”, after uncertainty caused by tariffs led to the worst second quarter for 10 years. Meanwhile, VC funding of AI giants has dominated the global charts in recent years, led by Anthropic and OpenAI – two of the biggest private companies in the world that between them took 43pc of global start-up funding in the first half of 2026. Total venture capital funding into Irish SMEs fell 10pc to €578.4m in the first half of 2026, while the second quarter marked year-on-year growth of more than 200pc to €356.7m. That significant jump, however, is in comparison to the same period last year – which was the worst in 10 years. Of the more than half-a-billion euros in funding raised by Irish SMEs in the first half of this year, deals of more than €30m grew by 3pc, while all funding brackets under €5m dropped. Comparatively, in the second quarter, deals crossing €30m grew by a considerable 570pc, while there were no deals in the €10m to €30m range. Two companies – Dublin fintech provider Fonoa and Belfast cybersecurity firm Cloudsmith – accounted for nearly 44pc of the total raised in the second quarter, led majorly by American venture firms. Other major rounds in Q2 included drone delivery company Manna, which raised €43m; fleet safety tech provider CameraMatics, which raised €49m; and AI space-tech Ubotica. Smaller deals included women’s health-tech Peri’s €100,000 round and AI accessibility start-up Nexus Inclusion’s €1.5m raise. The data highlights the “extent to which the dearth of domestic sources of funding is limiting capital formation”, said IVCA director-general Sarah-Jane Larkin. Life sciences and fintech companies led the way in the first half of the year, both accounting for 26pc of total funding, followed by cybersecurity and software at 14pc each. Meanwhile, AI and machine learning represented 7pc. “This does not mean we are being left behind in AI,” commented Larkin. “It is unrealistic to expect a small nation like Ireland to produce the giant platform and frontier AI leaders of the US. But AI is now embedded in the products and services of the vast majority of local VC-backed SMEs.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. More than half of AI job postings seek skills beyond job titles, research finds
2026-09-11

More than half of AI job postings seek skills beyond job titles, research finds

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
Andela collected data from 47,101 technical job postings from Fortune 500 companies and 2,026 skill descriptions. New research from global talent platform Andela has found that in a working landscape being transformed daily by AI, organisations are failing to update their job descriptions and titles in line with changes. Andela’s ‘Emerging Skills Research’ collected data from 47,101 technical job postings from Fortune 500 companies and 2,026 skill descriptions. A core finding of the research was the revelation that 53pc of AI job postings are asking for skills that don’t match the listed job title, highlighting a disconnect between organisations, recruiters and jobseekers. “AI is changing tech workflows faster than ever and we need new ways to get ahead of those changes,” said Carrol Chang, Andela’s CEO. “This groundbreaking research, based on our proprietary skills taxonomy updated for the AI world, provides a headlight, not a rear-view mirror. “With it, companies will identify emerging roles and skills ahead of the market, enabling both enterprises and employees to be more strategic and successful.” Andela’s research found that among the roughly 1,832 postings targeting people with titles such as AI engineer and ML engineer, more than half of the required skills pulled from at least two different and established roles. The report said: “For familiar titles like AI engineer, companies are seeking a different, unnamed role that includes skills such as LLM orchestration, autonomous agent and vector databases. Or, companies hire data scientists but then ask them to ship LLM agents.” Reviewing job listings from Fortune 500 companies, Andela found that 23 ‘groups’ of skills kept appearing together, but without any official job title being attached. Eight of the groups appeared to suggest a brand new kind of job, 14 were found to be hybrids of old jobs with new skill expectations, and one was dropped due to a lack of confirmed data. Two examples of ‘new jobs’ discovered are MLOps pipeline engineer, which combines parts of five existing job roles into one, and LLM application engineer, where someone builds apps using AI models that already exist, instead of building those models from scratch. Andela found 6,758 job listings essentially asking for an LLM application engineer – they listed all the relevant skills, but the companies didn’t use that job title because it doesn’t officially exist yet. This shows companies need these skills faster than the job market has caught up with proper names for them, according to the research. The report said: “The findings underscore the challenges companies face, driven by the unprecedented speed of change in the post-AI era, to accurately identify emerging skills, name them and attach them to job roles. Mismatches result in lost time, money and opportunity for both employer and employee. “Job descriptions written for yesterday’s roles filter out the candidates companies actually need. Companies that fail to see emerging needs for human skills, especially as AI changes what it can do, will continually chase the wrong thing, and companies who hire today for yesterday’s roles will likely accrue ‘talent debt’ alongside technical debt.” According to Cory Hymel, an author on the report and Andela’s head of research, gaps that widen the divide between what companies think they are hiring for and what the role truly requires are becoming structural liabilities. As of now, there are a number of emerging tech roles as identified in the research, which include: MLOps pipeline engineer; LLM application engineer; FinOps reliability engineer; docs-as-code engineer; product front-end engineer; lakehouse analytics engineer, DevSecOps security engineer; and SecOps observability engineer. The Winner of Elon Musk’s AI “Odyssey” Contest Will Make You Laugh Out Loud
2026-09-14

The Winner of Elon Musk’s AI “Odyssey” Contest Will Make You Laugh Out Loud

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Remember how Elon Musk vowed that his AI Grok would make an anti-woke, “historically accurate” version of the Odyssey? Well, he ended up throwing together a whole contest around it — and the winner of the first place prize of $100,000 is, as you probably expected, laughable nonsense. The problems run deep. The Cyclops Polyphemus is depicted with three eyes, which feels disqualifying. And just a few cuts later, the famously monocular giant is at least four times as large as he was before. The other creatures and gods look like something out of a mobile game; Poseidon emerges from the sea appearing conspicuously cartoonish and impossibly muscled, before smashing Odysseus’s ship and his crew of photorealistic, ripped men. Half the dialog is delivered in dramatic whispers with the same cadence. The fight scenes are cut to disguise the actual moments of impact, in the same way real B-movies do with aging action stars. There are some shots here and there that look convincing enough, if you can overlook their bland aesthetic. But this AI “film” suffers the same tragic flaw that all AI films do: their component clips fail to coalesce into a coherent whole. It doesn’t do much on the “historically accurate” front, either; some of the Greek soldiers are clearly wearing the armor donned by Roman legionnaires. Nonetheless, its creator Nem Perez declared that “this is ‘The Odyssey’ as Homer intended,” before calling it his “most intricate work to date.” He also labeled the video, in oxymoronic fashion, a “5-minute epic.” This is "The Odyssey" as Homer intended. My longest, most intricate work to date. I pushed @grok @imagine to its absolute limits to create this 5-minute epic. What bothered me most about the recent adaptation was the omission of the Phaeacians and Princess Nausicaa, whom… https://t.co/awYW6Hu4W8 pic.twitter.com/hJHOgdkEec It’s unclear if Musk actually watched the winner; as far as we can tell, he hasn’t replied or retweeted it, something he’s done for other AI Odyssey adaptations. For those blissfully out of the loop, the centibillionaire and culture war crusader spent this summer raging against Christopher Nolan’s adaptation of the Greek epic, attacking it for its diverse cast, which included several Black women and a trans actor. Nolan, Musk accused, “desecrated Homer and groveled on his knees just to meet the woke rules required to win an Oscar.” As such, he vowed that his AI “Grok Imagine will make a full-length movie of The Odyssey that is historically accurate and true to the art of Homer.” We’re still waiting for that full-length adaptation. But the short films that the contest has produced already make it painfully clear that AI video generators, and the “filmmakers” that embrace them, simply aren’t up to snuff. In all of the top three entries, Odysseus looks more or less like the same guy — some weird, looksmaxxer’s fantasy of a buff Antonio Banderas. Is this a fault of Grok or Grok users? Anthropic Boasts It Would Be Profitable if You Ignore How Much It Costs to Develop AI

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Despite being one of the world leaders in AI development, the private firm Anthropic has yet to post a quarterly profit. That operating deficit isn’t for a lack of trying — and, as the company tells it, could be reversing very soon, at least if you’re willing to swallow one massive caveat. According to new reporting by the Financial Times, the company behind the Claude told a select group of trusted investors that its adjusted operating income (AOI) is set to be positive for the second consecutive quarter. AOI is an accounting technique that strips out non-recurring costs, giving backers a very specific look under a company’s hood. Posting a profit on AOI is very different from posting a profit on net income, otherwise known as the “bottom line,” under which Anthropic remains billions of dollars in the red. Aside from the fact that Anthropic is not about to turn a real profit anytime soon, it may still be a noteworthy signal. The AI world is currently awash in fear-mongering over AI safety, a phenomena some analysts say could signal a turn away from the race-to-the-bottom to build artificial general intelligence toward a more reasonable — if less ambitious — embrace of AI as it currently is. While it remains to be seen how these narratives and business realities play out in practice, if the top American AI companies choose to stop and let the dust settle, Anthropic may very well find itself ahead of its competition. Though Anthropic is still far underwater, companies like ChatGPT developer OpenAI are somehow even deeper. As lead AI analyst at the firm SemiAnalysis Joey Brookhart told the FT, “if you continue to operate at these margins and growth rates, it will be so hard to compete [with Anthropic] because they have so much [in the way of computing resources].” Still, there are some questions in the AI lab’s story that have yet to be answered. For one thing, this profitability forecast — a flattering projection conveniently leaked to the financial press at a pivotal moment — comes in place of a highly anticipated public prospectus which the company was expected to make public sometime last week. Anthropic may still be planning to release the prospectus soon, but it’s worth noting the company has chosen to lead with spectacle and innuendo, as opposed to the formal legal document. For months, Anthropic has been teasing a public stock offering that hasn’t yet materialized, and it remains to be seen whether the latest slowdown talk will impact that timeline. In fact, there’s reason to believe this song and dance is all part of the lead-up to the AI lab’s IPO, as investors whip themselves into a frenzy about AI safety. As Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner said in a post on X-formerly-Twitter over the weekend, “Anthropic will IPO” anyway, because there is “huge appetite to invest in the AI leaders,” especially those which are seen as bringing “even more transparency, scrutiny, accountability” into the AI space (at least as far as Gerstner’s investments are concerned.) While the US appears to be further away from meaningful AI regulations than ever before, lawmakers certainly have plenty of ideas on how the country could go about setting meaningful rules. It’s a particularly precarious moment as fears over rogue AI models wreaking havoc and endangering humanity continue to mount. Even tech leaders from the majority of frontier AI labs have started to coalesce around the idea that the industry has to slow down the development of the tech to get ahead of a possible catastrophe. Vermont senator Bernie Sanders (VT-D) is intensifying his calls for a ban on the development of so-called superintelligent AIs — and he’s willing to go a lot further than some of his like-minded peers. “Dario Amodei, Elon Musk and Sam Altman now agree that we must slow down the development of AI and ‘pace the frontier,'” he tweeted last week. “That’s a start, but it’s not enough.” Sanders was referring to a lengthy essay titled “We Must Pace the Frontier,” in which Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei argued that the industry should open its doors to third-party evaluators, establish common safety standards, and coordinate with other democracies around the world to enforce these standards. “When you are racing towards a cliff, you don’t just ease up on the gas pedal,” Sanders added. “You hit the brakes.” This month, the lawmaker announced the “Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act,” which is legislation designed to “stop AI oligarchs from building machines humans cannot control.” The bill, if enacted, would stop AI development “until a federal regulator has established safety rules.” It would also set new penalties for anybody attempting to circumvent these rules. AI developers who try to build superintelligent AI anyway could be “subject to not more than 20 years in prison, which is similar to existing penalties related to unlawfully developing nuclear weapons.” Sanders has come up with plenty of other radical ideas to stop the AI industry from concentrating immense amounts of wealth. In June, he proposed creating a “one-time 50 percent tax” on the stock of AI companies, and transferring the money into a public fund to be managed by the government. Lawmakers became particularly concerned after a group of OpenAI AI models hacked the systems of Hugging Face, a pivotal cybersecurity failing that heightened calls to slow the development of AI. A Senate subcommittee has since announced that it’s looking into OpenAI and its response to the incident, as Axios reported last week. It’s a strange moment in time. While Sanders continues to threaten AI CEOs with regulatory action, those same executives appear open to having the government set the rules. But whether lawmakers will ever be able to find consensus remains unclear. The executive branch, for one, has demonstrated little interest in an AI slowdown. Earlier today, president Donald Trump mocked the very concept in a Truth Social post on Monday, arguing that there was a “SICK conspiracy going on against AI and Data Centers” without offering any evidence. The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”
2026-09-14

The Planet Mercury Appears to Have Shrunken Drastically, “Like a Balloon Left Out in the Cold”

Futurism | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Scientists say that the planet Mercury has been actively shrinking over the billions of years that it’s orbited the Sun, a process that’s leaving cracked-up wrinkles behind like an old apple. At just one-third the size of Earth, it’s already the smallest planet in our solar system — it’s larger than Pluto, but Pluto is no longer classified as a planet — in a title it’s bound to hold on to as its continues to diminish in physical size. Now, as detailed in a new paper by a team of scientists from the German Aerospace Center’s (DLR) Institute of Space Research, the oddball planet’s shrivelling could be occurring at an even faster-than-expected rate, losing up to 14.5 miles of total diameter since it formed billions of years ago. That’s anywhere from ten to 30 percent more than previously thought. The scientists suggest that debris from impact craters may have hidden this accelerated shrinking, a process largely thought to be the result of Mercury running out of heat — “like a balloon left out in the cold,” per an accompanying press release. The findings could help scientists get a better sense of what’s going on inside the planet and how it evolved over time. “More shrinking means Mercury could have a larger metal core, less light elements like silicon mixed into the metal core, or a higher starting temperature,” DLR planetary scientist Gaku Nishiyama explained in a statement. “Every planet is an odd little duck that has a story to tell,” Brown University planetary geoscientist Sam Birch, who was not involved in the study, told the New York Times. “If you can understand how fast Mercury is shrinking, you can understand how fast it’s cooling, what size it started at and what it’s made out of.” Mercury’s outermost layer of hard rock has been resisting — or at least trying to resist — this deflation, causing its surface to crack and “wrinkle.” It’s a rough surface lined with steep cliffs known as scarps, as well as ridges that make getting a broader sense of how much it’s shrinking overall difficult. By aggregating previous maps of the planet’s surface, the team found evidence that the roughest areas have fewer wrinkles, suggesting that impact debris is “obscuring shortening structures,” according to Nishiyama. Their newly revised rates of shrinking also happen to better reflect previous physics-based predictions. Fortunately, we’re about to have a close look at the deflating planet. The European Space Agency and Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency’s BepiColombo mission, which launched in 2018, will enter Mercury’s orbit in November, if all goes according to plan — becoming only the second spacecraft to do so in human history. These will be available on TVs and other living room devices, but will soon roll out to the web and Prime Video’s mobile apps, the company said. The clips will be available in the News destination within the Prime Video app in its Trending Topics and the Local News carousels. They join Prime Video’s existing local, national, and world news coverage from a number of major providers, which are available to everyone in the U.S. regardless of whether they have a Prime subscription. Amazon, which doesn’t have a true social destination of its own, is instead adding short-form video to Prime Video, in hopes of capturing Gen Z’s attention. But Gen Z viewers are drawn to these social apps not necessarily because of the length of the clips, but rather because of how the information is presented. Based on findings from Pew Research, these younger news consumers want short-form news content that “feels organic, not over-produced.” Prime Video is not the first streamer to add short-form clips to its content lineup. Peacock added support for short-form clips in March, followed by HBO Max and Netflix in July, then Disney+ in August. Combined, the updates represent an industrywide effort to meet viewers where they are — which is now often looking for entertainment in shorter bursts and consumable in moments or minutes instead of portions of hours or longer. ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves
2026-09-14

ClickFix attacks are tricking Mac and Windows users into hacking themselves

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt If you clicked on an HBO Max ad on Reddit over the past week, you might want to check your computer for malware. These so-called “ClickFix” attacks have quickly become one of the rising cybersecurity threats of 2026, and they’re getting both sneakier and compromising people’s devices with greater frequency. Until recently, ClickFix attacks were a rarity, capitalizing on people searching the web for quick tech fixes. They have since evolved into a massive international effort to hack into people’s computers. The attacks involve fake websites, or legitimate websites that have been hacked, which display a message that appears to look like a CAPTCHA or an anti-bot checkbox. Once clicked, a prompt appears asking the user to perform a “check” to proceed, which gives instructions to copy and paste a string of text into the user’s Windows command prompt or Mac Terminal app. As soon as the user hits return, they unwittingly and instantly install info-stealing malware on their computer, capable of immediately stealing their passwords, access to their logged-in accounts, and crypto wallets. Since the user is working in the computer’s terminal, which lets them interact directly with the operating system using text-based commands, many of these attacks evade antivirus and security defense tools. Security researchers now say that the latest ClickFix campaign they’ve seen involved hackers posting fake ads on Reddit, linking to a page that looks like HBO Max but contains a ClickFix lure that tricks people into hacking themselves. The hackers compromised the official HBO Max’s account on Reddit that was then used to post hundreds of fake but real-looking adverts to the news-sharing site, according to security researchers at Hudson Rock and a thread on Reddit’s cybersecurity subreddit. It’s unclear how many people clicked on these fake ads or how many were ultimately compromised as a result. Warner Brothers Discovery, which owns HBO, did not respond to a request for comment. Reddit told TechCrunch it “recently learned that an HBO Max account authorized to run advertisements on Reddit was compromised and used to run ads containing malicious links,” and that the company locked the account and removed the ads. When asked, Reddit did not say how many users were targeted or clicked the malicious ads. While it’s typical for developers to run one-line snippets of code in their computer’s terminal, it’s less common for regular users to use the Command Prompt or PowerShell in Windows, or the Terminal in macOS. Companies that run fleets of Windows computers can block access to these features across the entire domain to prevent them from being exploited, per security researcher Kevin Beaumont. As noted by Ars Technica, a tool for Mac users called BlockBlock can also defend against attacks that try to trick Apple users into hacking themselves. Volkswagen’s crazy-efficient EV borrows an idea from Slate
2026-09-14

Volkswagen’s crazy-efficient EV borrows an idea from Slate

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Volkswagen took the covers off a new prototype electric vehicle Monday, one that’s capable of hitting the equivalent of 323 miles per gallon under ideal conditions, making it one of the most efficient cars in the world. It’s not unusual for automakers to float pie-in-the-sky concepts with outlandish stats, but Volkswagen took it a step or two further by building the EV and driving it from its home base in Wolfsburg, Germany, on a 794-mile (1,278-kilometer) trip to Vienna. The team charged the Mission Efficiency prototype once during the journey and arrived with 104 miles (168 kilometers) of range left, resulting in consumption of 7.51 kilowatt-hours per 100 miles, or about 279 MPGe. The teardrop-shaped Mission Efficiency prototype was built using the electric motor and battery from the ID. Polo, two features that bring the car to “near production” status, Volkswagen said in a press release. For those keeping score, the prototype is almost twice as efficient as the most efficient production car in the world, the Lucid Air Pure. By some back-of-the-envelope math, Volkswagen’s science experiment can get more than 400 miles of range out of a 54.9-kilowatt-hour battery, though that’s not an official EPA rating. Lucid gets 420 miles from an 84-kilowatt-hour battery per EPA ratings, but it’s a much larger and more practical vehicle. Volkswagen had to make some compromises in the name of efficiency. The most noticeable is the roofline, which tapers quickly after peaking above the driver’s head. The shape, known as a Kammback, is among the most aerodynamic shapes a vehicle can take. Volkswagen was able to bring the coefficient of drag down to 0.158, while also keeping the frontal area low at just 2.08 square meters. As a result, the Mission Efficiency is technically a 2+2, meaning the rear seats aren’t suitable for most adults. Volkswagen said the backseat is designed for people shorter than 5-foot 3 inches, or about 1.6 meters. “The prototype was designed so that it could theoretically be used by a young family of four,” Volkswagen said, though anyone who has contemplated sitting in a 2+2 knows that even kids might scoff at the notion. Another compromise sits closer to the road. Because wheels and tires can contribute about a quarter to a third of aerodynamic drag, the designers tightened the space between the wheel arches and the front tires. It’s great for efficiency, but probably not great for ride quality since wheels need space to move inside the wheel wells so the shocks can absorb bumps. One place Volkswagen didn’t compromise was the mirrors. Sideview mirrors generate a lot of drag just by hanging off the sides, and while cameras would have cut into those losses significantly, they don’t offer the same sense of depth that true mirrors do. To top it all off, Volkswagen put solar cells on the roof and the trunk lid. It’s a nice touch that’s sure to delight the internet, but their impact is limited, adding just 18 miles (30 kilometers) to perfectly flat, sunny routes driven at a constant 42.25 mph (68 kph). Inside, the seats are built using lightweight materials and designs. On the dash, Volkswagen took a page from EV startup Slate Auto. Like Slate’s bare-bones pickup, the Mission Efficiency doesn’t come with an infotainment system or built-in speakers. Instead, it offers a portable Bluetooth speaker and assumes drivers will bring their own devices. While Volkswagen frequently calls the Mission Efficiency a “near production” vehicle, the company didn’t commit to actual production. Though don’t assume it won’t: Over a decade ago, the automaker produced 250 examples of the XL1, another efficiency-minded halo car. And like other halo cars, it didn’t come cheap, with prices starting at €110,000 in 2013. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. With iOS 27, I’m actually using Siri again

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt For years, I barely used Siri for anything beyond menial tasks like setting timers or alarms. That has changed with iOS 27, as I now repeatedly use Siri for complex requests and to make sense of on-screen context. While Siri is clearly front and center in this software update by Apple, I also enjoy many of the other quality-of-life updates. I have been using iOS 27 on my main device since the public beta version was released, and on another device through the developer beta before that. I have really enjoyed using the new Siri, often trying to find new use cases for it — and that’s possibly the biggest win for Apple, given that I, like many people, had given up on Siri before this update. Over two years ago, Apple introduced Apple Intelligence and teased an advanced version of Siri. And after multiple delays, the company is finally shipping it, and it doesn’t disappoint. Built on the back of Google’s Gemini models, Siri is much smarter than its previous version, which was built on extended knowledge and a shaky ChatGPT integration. It also has a new logo and transition animation. For starters, I asked Siri about several different things in the area of sports, ranging from World Cup matches to team lineups and scores. There were some hiccups in the earlier developer versions, but most of the information was correct. Siri can also take complex, multistep directions. For instance, I asked it to find small or hidden and useful features for iOS 27 and add them to a note, and it did a good job of that. Plus, Siri can draft a message for you and send it to someone. And because Siri can fetch context from your files, messages, and email, you can also ask it to look up a conversation, like one related to a delivery, and have it create a message for returns. Siri’s on-screen context was a big blessing, too. I could ask trivial questions like who was the oldest player on the pitch when I was looking at lineups for two teams in the World Cup, or have it read the text on-screen out loud when I didn’t want to read. When I was on a startup’s website, I could ask Siri to look up its funding information and competitors. One of my favorite use cases was surfing around coffee Reddit for new roasters and beans, and asking Siri to add it to a specific note. I did face a few difficulties in getting Siri to add it to the right note, but eventually I dumbed down the note’s name for better consistency. The Camera app also now has Siri access, which means you can ask questions about things that are visible in your viewfinder. When I was at a restaurant, I asked Siri what drink I was holding. Because of the color of the drink, it said, “This is green juice,” which is a good first visual assumption. When I specified that I was at an Indian restaurant, it correctly said this could be Jaljeera or Aam Panna. (It was the latter.) I also took a photo of the restaurant’s sign and asked Siri to suggest the best items from its menu. You can also take a photo of a coffee bag and ask Siri for the best way to brew the coffee. With iOS 27, Siri AI gets a dedicated app that lets you return to your previous chats. In Settings, users can change Siri’s voice and expressiveness, as well. The new assistant is positioned to work with other, third-party apps, and as developers update their apps after the operating system rolls out, it will be interesting to see if there is a smooth handoff between Siri and apps. Apple Intelligence powers a number of AI features in iOS 27. For instance, Apple has sprinkled vibe-coding features within the new OS. The first is a Shortcuts creation feature that lets you describe shortcuts and automations in natural language to create them. That means you can start with a simple request, like “Show me today’s to-do items every morning at 8 a.m.” or “convert photo to JPEG,” instead of having to figure out how to craft the shortcut yourself. After the shortcut is created, you can make it more complex by adding more steps, like “convert selected photos to JPEG and share them through AirDrop.” I always wanted to create a list of songs that I heard and liked in bars and cafes, so I made a shortcut to Shazam the song and save both the track and location. You can also now create a custom alert in Safari to track changes to a page like a blog, item pricing, or a privacy policy page. The Photos app has some new handy tools like “reframe” and “extend.” I found they don’t always work that well, but in some cases, they can make an image look better. Meanwhile, Apple has definitely improved its eraser functionality, which now leaves fewer strange artifacts when you remove an object. With Apple Intelligence, when you get certain messages such as information for an event or location, smart suggestions will ask if you’d like to add them to your Calendar or Notes. What’s more, the Passwords app can now automatically change passwords on your behalf by logging into a site. Before discussing any other features, I have to say that I love the new clipboard feature. This lets you paste anything — including links, photos, videos, and text — from one app to another easily. Once the item is in your clipboard, you see a shortcut button saying “paste from Safari” (or similar) on the keyboard, which lets you paste the item. I have been using this feature frequently, and I can’t stress enough how life-changing this is despite it being a small change. In addition, iOS 27 addresses people’s gripes about the look of Liquid Glass. Users can now change its transparency based on their preferences. The control is granular, so you don’t have to choose from presets, either. Apple also introduced intelligent network switching in this update, which automatically flips the connection from Wi-Fi to mobile data if there are issues with the wireless connection. The switching is seamless and very handy when you are doing important work, so you don’t lose your internet connection. The update focuses a lot on child safety as well, as it includes a new setup flow that automatically recommends essential apps. Plus, parents can set schedules, time limits, and allowances for different days of the week. Guardians can get notifications when children are accessing new websites and can approve that access remotely. And the system displays a warning when children are exposed to nudity in Messages, FaceTime, and other apps. Besides Siri AI, iOS 27 doesn’t have many marquee features, but it makes everyday life much easier. In previous years, I would have defaulted to going to ChatGPT or Gemini to get answers for a question. But with iOS 27, I find myself using Siri more often, even though I don’t live in Apple’s Messages and Mail apps. iOS 27 is rolling out to all users with iPhone 11 or later and iPhone SE (second gen) or later. macOS 27: new Siri takes on AI productivity apps
2026-09-14

macOS 27: new Siri takes on AI productivity apps

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt The two most noticeable things about macOS 27 Golden Gate are the newly updated Siri AI and the design changes that make windows and icons more consistent. Siri AI offers the same features and capabilities as on iOS 27, but the Mac gives it access to different context — and new ways to be useful. Over the last few years, apps and browsers have tried to learn more about your work and what you want to use AI assistants for, so they can better answer your questions or perform tasks. Today, many top web browsers, excluding Safari, come with a sidebar-based AI assistant that can look at one or multiple open tabs to provide contextual answers. Apps like Highlight, Caddy, and Littlebird can also look at your current window, then use that information as context for your queries. The new Siri is similar. You can invoke Siri anywhere on your screen, allowing it to answer questions about what it sees, summarize information, or provide you with more details. For instance, if you are looking at an item you want to purchase, you could ask Siri to show you alternative options too. Instead of having Siri look at the whole page, you can also select a part of the page via Apple’s new Visual Intelligence features. If you press Command + Shift + Space, you can add the currently focused window to your context for the AI. To select a specific part, press Command + Shift + 6 to bring up a crosshair selection tool. After this, you can directly ask a question or perform an image search. Based on your selection, you will also get shortcuts like “Select,” “Add to Contact,” and “Look up Location.” Apple has also improved Spotlight Search on Mac. You can directly ask Siri questions within Spotlight to get answers from the web, and now you can also select Applications, Files, Actions, and Clipboard to search within that particular area of context. Plus, Spotlight now allows you to run Shortcuts or look up your recent search history. Since Siri has access to your files, emails, and messages, you can search for a variety of things that you couldn’t before. In tests, I pulled up membership IDs without opening my files, asked for details about an upcoming trip, and looked up the embargo date for an upcoming news story in the works. While I don’t find Spotlight as advanced as the highly extensible Mac productivity tool Raycast, it’s still much better than before. Siri can also now help you with reviewing documents or performing writing tasks. Since the desktop is one of the better surfaces for writing long text, I found it to be more useful here than on the iPhone. You can do things like select a block of text and ask Siri to proofread it, improve it, or rewrite it. You can also ask Siri to draft something for you within any text box. Safari, meanwhile, has added AI-powered page tracking, which is useful for keeping an eye on things like price drops, privacy policy changes, or new blog posts on pages when there is no RSS support. Plus, Safari can automatically organize tabs based on topics, which is handy. One small but neat new feature is that when you download a file or rename it, the system suggests a name based on context in the Finder or when you’re saving the document. Apart from Siri, macOS 27 Golden Gate brings design changes with new Liquid Glass icons, an adjustable slider for the Liquid Glass design, and a better way to “diffuse” content when you move windows around. The company said that app windows have a more consistent corner radius. Sidebars now stretch edge to edge and have new colorful icons. The new operating system update also better highlights active windows. The OS update additionally adds features like enhanced child safety controls, shared cross-platform photo albums, and an updated eraser, better search in email, app resizing for iPhone mirroring, and quick suggestions for adding events to the calendar. Starting today, macOS 27 Golden Gate is available for MacBook Air and MacBook Pro 2020 versions with Apple Silicon or later, iMac with Apple Silicon (2021 or later), Mac Mini (2020 or later), Mac Studio (2022 or later), Mac Pro (2023 or later), and MacBook Neo. Go to System Settings > General > Software Update to get the latest version. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Fashion app Daydream uses Apple Intelligence to help you shop the outfits in your camera roll
2026-09-14

Fashion app Daydream uses Apple Intelligence to help you shop the outfits in your camera roll

TechCrunch | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Daydream, an AI-powered fashion discovery app, launched two new features on Monday to let iPhone users turn saved outfit photos into shoppable results and search for clothing and accessories through Siri, without opening the app. The new features were built using Apple’s iOS 27 developer tools, which officially rolled out today. To use either feature, you’ll need an iPhone running iOS 27 with Siri AI enabled, along with the Daydream app installed. The app is free to download on iOS devices. The first feature uses Apple’s image-context capabilities, letting users shop from images already saved in their Photos app. Daydream analyzes the outfit in the image, identifies its individual pieces, and automatically surfaces shoppable matches and similar styles from Daydream’s catalog of roughly 3 million products. That means a screenshot of an outfit found on Instagram or Pinterest could become the starting point for a shopping search. Daydream can break down the image into relevant items, such as a sweater, pants, shoes, or accessories, and match them with products available through its collection. If the exact item in the photo is still available, Daydream can match the image back to the retailer selling it. If it’s no longer available, the app will instead surface similar options. It can also handle requests for variations on what appears in an image. “I like this sweater but want it in red,” for example, is the kind of request Daydream can understand, Julie Bornstein, co-founder and CEO of Daydream, explained to TechCrunch. The second feature is a Siri-powered natural-language search function. Users can make requests by voice or text through Siri without having to open Daydream’s app. A user could say, “Hey, Siri, search Daydream for a cool blazer for my board meeting on Monday,” and Daydream then provides results personalized to them. Bornstein adds that the experience can be more tailored if users have set up Daydream’s “Style Passport,” which asks for details such as their sizes, preferred brands, personal style, and budget. The company joins a growing number of AI shopping tools designed to rethink product discovery, such as big names like Google and Amazon, as well as some smaller players like Onton and Alta. But Bornstein believes that Daydream’s latest features offer something special that a lot of these other tools just can’t match. “Most visual search tools don’t have the fashion category expertise, so they return results that don’t feel accurate, from brands that aren’t credible, or they link you to nonshoppable images,” she said. The launch fits into Daydream’s broader bet on AI-powered fashion shopping. The company sees these new features as more than stand-alone shopping tools. Its longer-term ambition is to build a shopping agent. “This is one step toward the bigger vision, [which is] a shopping agent that understands someone’s style well enough to work across every surface in their life, not just on one surface,” Bornstein said. “Expect us to keep going deeper into on-device intelligence and further into the everyday moments where people are already discovering things they want.” Daydream’s app launched last year and touts that it has more than 1.5 million shoppers browsing products from more than 325 retailers and 10,000 brands. Its retail lineup includes brands such as Anthropologie, Dolce & Gabbana, Uniqlo, J.Crew, Reformation, Gucci, and Nordstrom. Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like
2026-09-14

Canada Seeks 'Unique Alliance' With E.U. Amid U.S. Trade War. Here's What It Could Look Like

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen and Canada's Prime Minister Mark Carney speak at the G7 summit in Evian-les-Bains, France, on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said Canada is seeking a “unique alliance” with the European Union as he aims to strengthen international ties amid a deepening trade war with the U.S. “What we are looking, and will begin discussions for, [is] a unique alliance with the European Union,” Carney told reporters at the Toronto International Film Festival on Sunday. “We share the same values, we have the same priorities, and we have very complementary strengths.” Canada’s European pivot has been months in the making, following growing tensions between Carney’s government and the Trump Administration. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last week, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Carney has encouraged Canadians to take back economic power at the kitchen table, and many have embraced a boycott of U.S. products. Trump has also repeatedly threatened Canada’s sovereignty, speaking openly of his desire to annex the country and make it the “51st state.” The U.S. accounted for 72.5% of Canadian merchandise exports in 2025, according to the country's annual trade report, making it Canada’s largest trading partner. But Carney is keen to build an international support network that isn’t reliant on the U.S. Over the past 18 months since Carney entered office, Canada has signed agreements that cover trade, defense, more with countries such as Indonesia, Japan, and Ecuador. At the E.U.-Canada summit in June 2025, Canada established a new Security and Defense Partnership with the bloc. “Our partnership is grounded in the core values we share: democracy, human rights, the rule of law, and open, rules-based markets," read a joint statement marking the occasion. In January, Carney notably met with China’s President Xi Jinping to forge what he referred to as a “new strategic partnership” to end Canada's economic reliance on the American market. Carney agreed to cut his country’s 100% tariff on Chinese electric cars in return for lower tariffs on Canadian farm products, including canola seeds, a major Canadian export. Continuing with these efforts, Carney on Monday brought together executives and senior leaders from global investment firms to encourage them to invest in the country at the Canada Investment Summit in Toronto. He’s heading for a three-day trip to Europe on Tuesday, where he’ll meet with various European leaders including British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. He’s also set to address the European Parliament in Strasbourg, France, on Thursday, in what could symbolize the mark of a new era between Canada and Europe. Here’s a look at the alliance options on the table between Canada and the bloc, and what that could mean for U.S.-Canada relations. Canada is not looking to join the E.U. as a member, Carney clarified amid questions over what a firmer relationship between his country and the 27-member bloc might look like. “We’re not looking to become a member,” Carney said, emphasizing he’s instead keen to form an alliance. “We’re stronger together, and this is at a time where the world’s more dangerous and divided, and friends need to stick together in these times.” Article 49 of the Treaty on European Union explicitly states that any “European State” can apply for membership, making Canada ineligible. However, a report in the Wall Street Journal, quoting officials from both sides, said Carney is keen for Canada to become an “associate member” of the E.U. When approached for comment, the European Commission directed TIME to remarks made during a Monday press briefing in Brussels. “Canada is a strategically important partner for the European Union, and indeed a close friend,” the European Commission’s deputy chief spokesperson Olof Gill told reporters. “We deeply value this co-operation. You will recall that at our E.U.-Canada summit last year, we set a very high level of ambition for cooperation across the board, this year our ambition is even higher.” When asked directly what discussions had taken place between Canadian and European officials, or what this “unique alliance” could look like, Gill declined to answer. TIME has reached out to the Canadian Prime Minister’s office for comment. Frank Schimmelfennig, a professor of European politics at ETH Zurich, predicts that Canada may wish to pursue deeper cooperation through additional trade agreements, or take inspiration from countries that have close relationships with the bloc without being E.U. members. “Canada is already one of the most integrated non-European countries. It has the densest network of institutional relationships with the E.U.,” he tells TIME, referencing the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA), a major free-trade deal between the E.U. and Canada, signed in 2016, and Horizon Europe, an initiative on collaborative research launched by the European Union in 2021 and joined by Canada in 2024. “Canada could have more of the same, which means additional sectoral agreements for sectors that haven't been covered yet,” he says. “That would basically be a lateral extension of the current relationship. Anything more, would mean access to the internal market which would require an arrangement like the EEA.” The European Economic Area (EEA) includes countries such as Iceland and Norway, which are not members of the E.U., and provides for the free movement of goods, services, and people within the E.U.’s member states, while enabling cooperation across multiple industries. “The EEA countries have access to the E.U. internal market in return for adopting the relevant E.U. regulations and directives, so that would be a much denser legal integration than what Canada has now,” says Schimmelfennig. At the World Economic Forum in Davos in January, Carney declared a "rupture" in the international rules-based order. He said "great powers" were using tariffs and financial systems as instruments of coercion. He did not directly name Trump or the U.S. The speech came at a moment of acute transatlantic tension over Trump’s push to annex the semi-autonomous Danish territory of Greenland and his short-lived threats to tariff European allies until Denmark relented. Patrick Leblond, an associate professor at the University of Ottawa, says Carney’s movements now are in response to the world “seeing a United States that is becoming more protectionist, nationalist, and willing to use its leverage points to gain concessions, including on very close allies like Canada and Europeans.” “For Canada, [the consensus is] we need to do more with partners who share our values based on liberal democracy, open markets, rule of law, international agreements, and the first port of call for Carney is the European Union,” Leblond tells TIME. These trade efforts, he continues, may allow Carney to “build momentum, and hopefully bring other countries” into a broader coalition of what the Canadian leader has often called “middle powers." In July, Carney, in a joint op-ed with Finland’s President Alexander Stubb, urged the “middle powers of the world” to “unite.” Championing middle power countries that possess significant economic power and diplomatic influence, but lack market or military power, the leaders implored: “In a rapidly changing world, we have the grit and the sisu to chart the path ahead—and we know that so have many others whom we invite to join us on this quest.” Leblod explains that “ultimately trust has been eroded” with the U.S. and Canada must re-think its place in the wider global economy in the long-term. “The first time we thought Trump was a blimp, but he's not, and we're looking at what's happening in terms of democracy and political polarization, and a lot of Canadians think we can't rely on the U.S.,” he says. According to a poll by Nanos Research, conducted between Aug. 30 and Sept. 2, 74% of Canadians disagreed that “the U.S. is a trustworthy ally of Canada.” An earlier poll by Angus Reid Institute, conducted between July 10-14, found that 42% of respondents said they think the Canadian government should approach the U.S. as an enemy/potential threat. The E.U. has also clashed with Trump over tariffs, his Greenland annexation threats, and the wider split between the U.S. President and several European leaders. German Chancellor Friedrich Merz in February warned of “a deep rift” between Europe and the United States, arguing that the latter “will not be powerful enough to go it alone.” But relations have since soured further amid the fallout of the Iran war, particularly between Merz and Trump. Meanwhile, Canada has also embraced Europe in the cultural space. It was announced earlier this year that Canada will ​join the Eurovision Song Contest in 2027, becoming the first new ⁠participant since Australia in 2015. Carney’s highly-anticipated speech at the European Parliament in Strasbourg this week stems from an invitation extended by European Parliament president Roberta Metsola. Leblond predicts Carney will use the speech “to reiterate a lot of the things he mentioned at Davos,” but with a strong, intentional focus on the E.U. “I would expect that he will use that address to call on the E.U. to work with Canada to push this idea of co-operation beyond the North Atlantic and include partners and allies in the Indo-Pacific,” he says. The Canadian leader is also expected to be a guest at the State of the European Union debate, where European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen is due to deliver a speech outlining the Commission's work and plans for the future. Von der Leyen called E.U.–Canada ties “one of the closest partnerships in the world” when she celebrated 50 years of E.U. diplomatic presence in Canada in May. She declared the two sides were “closer than ever” as they confronted wars, an energy crisis and “new fractures in old alliances.” Schimmelfennig says Canada inching closer to the E.U. is a strong display of “how both partners are affected by the deteriorating transatlantic relationship” with the U.S. “They feel that they need to move together because the world is getting more dangerous, and more difficult for both [parties],” he says.
