In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex
2026-09-14

In Gen Z Romance Shows Like Crew Girl, Sports Are the New Sex

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From left: Sam Braun, Miku Martineau, and Kyle Clark in Crew Girl Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the Netflix teen drama Crew Girl, 16-year-old solo rowing champ Teagan Tao has her life uprooted when her hotel-magnate dad gets a tip that he’s about to be arrested for financial crimes and disappears. Suddenly, Teagan (Miku Martineau) and her flighty mom, Ella (Jessica Paré), are broke and moving across the country to the crumbling New England house where Ella grew up. The town is all clam shacks and marinas—and the prestigious Easton Prep, where Teagan matriculates thanks to an education trust that was somehow spared from the seizure of the Taos’ assets. The only problem, besides the rampant snobbery, is that Eaton has no girls’ crew team. Forced to coxswain for the boys, Teagan starts falling for two of her teammates: kind, working-class townie Cam Dillinger (Kyle Clark) and bratty airline scion Josh Regis (Sam Braun), who’s locked in a premature succession battle with his cousin Ryder (Connor Paton). Meanwhile, a gay rower, Baloo (Jude Wilson), is secretly hooking up with a guy on a rival team. All of which is to say that Crew Girl, whose first season is now the no. 1 show on Netflix, plays like a greatest-hits compilation of teen romance tropes. First among the classics is a love triangle (see: Twilight, Riverdale, The Vampire Diaries, My So-Called Life). As is often the case, this one is enhanced by the quaintness of its small-town setting (Dawson’s Creek, One Tree Hill) and complicated by both wealth disparities (Gossip Girl, Pretty in Pink) and one character’s fish-out-of-water, new-kid-in-school status (The O.C., Beverly Hills, 90210). There’s also a youthful mom (you might remember Paré as Mad Men’s Megan) doing a painfully slow will-they-won’t-they dance with an old friend (Thomas Cadrot), à la Gilmore Girls’ Lorelai and Luke. From left: Jarrett Carlington, Jude Wilson, Connor Paton, Kyle Clark, and Miku Martineau in Crew Girl Netflix But what’s remarkable about Crew Girl—which is, despite an effervescent lead and a few fun storylines built around eccentric minor crew-boy characters, otherwise decidedly mid—is how comprehensively it showcases the conventions unique to the genre’s newest generation. TV has, in the last few years, capitalized on publishing’s pandemic-era romance boom, churning out love stories tailored to the young, female audience driving authors like Emily Henry and Ali Hazelwood up best-seller lists. Often, that means adapting popular YA or New Adult novels; Prime Video’s success with Jenny Han’s coming-of-age, beach-town love triangle The Summer I Turned Pretty paved the way for Netflix’s take on Ali Novak’s My Life with the Walter Boys as well as XO, Kitty, Han’s spinoff of her book-to-film franchise To All the Boys I’ve Loved Before. Queer romance, mainly of the gay male variety, from Netflix’s tender and chaste Heartstopper to the spicy Canadian phenomenon Heated Rivalry, which streams on HBO Max in the U.S., has also jumped from page to screen. (Crew Girl isn’t an adaptation, but a novelization released in tandem with the show suggests it’s meant to feel like one.) This year has seen a particular glut of new titles, from Netflix’s figure-skating drama Finding Her Edge to Prime’s Off Campus and Every Year After. The latter platform even scored a hit in August with an exceptional original teen romance, Sterling Point, proving the niche isn’t entirely dependent on preexisting IP. These shows have all the love triangles and class struggles and outsider angst that have defined youthful romance tales since Jane Austen was writing them, capturing eternal adolescent fantasies (being desirable enough to have two people fighting over you) and fears (not measuring up to the rich kids) alike. Pretty appears to have sparked a mini-trend of siblings competing for the same crush, a trope that also pops up in Every Year After, Walter Boys, and Finding Her Edge. The fraught post-DEI-backlash politics of representation, especially for the most racially and ethnically diverse generation on record, are evident in the shows’ leads. Like Crew Girl’s Martineau, many are multiracial but play characters with vaguely defined backgrounds who live with either a single white parent or a white surrogate family. (New York magazine just spotlighted the "Wasian generation," a long list of up-and-coming white and Asian stars, citing Heated Rivalry’s Hudson Williams, Pretty’s Lola Tung, and XO, Kitty’s Anna Cathcart among other white and Asian stars.) Liberal and minority viewers get to see mixed-race families and romances, and a TV landscape that isn’t all white. The anti-”woke” crowd is spared identity politics, non-English dialogue, and often all other forms of cultural specificity. Savvy streamers avoid backlash from both sides. Connor Storrie, left, and Hudson Williams in Heated Rivalry HBO Max What’s most striking about these series, though, are the novel ways in which they embrace physicality. With the steamy exceptions of Heated Rivalry and Off Campus (both geared toward a slightly older age range), the sexual content is pretty PG-13. Objectification is kept to a minimum, at least by characters we’re supposed to like. Yet bodies are conspicuously activated, in what feel like pointed responses to—and, for young viewers, escape hatches from—digital disembodiment. Summer romances, and specifically those that bring together wealthy vacationers and the locals who work for them, aren’t new to fiction. It’s the prominence of nature in the recent crop of teen romances (and teen hits in other genres, like Netflix’s Outer Banks) that makes an impression. As it toggles between protagonist Percy’s (Sadie Soverall) life in her late 20s and a sequence of idyllic teenage lake-house summers glimpsed in flashbacks, Every Year After uses its setting to visualize two brothers’ rivalry for her affection. In one recurring image, Percy sits at the edge of her house’s dock, looking on as sensitive Sam (Matt Cornett) and arrogant Charlie (Michael Bradway) shove each other off a slippery wooden raft. Sterling Point brings a high-strung New York City girl (Ella Rubin) to an island in Canada’s Muskoka region, a paradise of lush, lakefront forests. Her rich-vs.-poor-boy love triangle plays out over lazy days spent boating, swimming, and sunbathing on rocky beaches. Cellphone service is conveniently spotty. An abundance of sports romances make such physicality even more explicit. In Off Campus and Heated Rivalry, the boys who serve as eye candy for any viewer attracted to men are always slamming into each other on the ice and changing together in the locker room. In Finding Her Edge, a show about young Olympics hopefuls at a residential training center, an ice-dancing duo is bound to become a love affair (as long as the guy is into girls). Even the neurotic hero of Heartstopper (Joe Locke) finds first love on the rugby field. Crew Girl casts many thirsty gazes on the musclebound upper bodies of Teagan’s teammates. These stories aren’t just about the sweaty sensuality of athletes in motion, though. They’re about ambition and passion and the competitive spirit as romantic accelerants—about how the shared commitment to spend hours every day striving for excellence while one’s peers are screwing around online can bond people. Belmont Cameli and Ella Bright in Off Campus Liane Hentscher—Prime Why use athletics as a fig leaf for sex in the uncensored age of streaming, years after shows like Euphoria and Skins obliterated the notion that TV for teens should be wholesome? Maybe because modesty is in. Gen Z’s squeamishness about sex on screen has fueled successive waves of hand-wringing among their elders. The so-called sex recession plaguing this cohort in real life has been duly noted, with suggested explanations ranging from extreme onlineness in general to poor socialization during the COVID years of Zoom school and virtual prom in particular. The thing is, biologically speaking, teens are the same jumble of hormones in 2026 that they were during the Summer of Love. “Many of them are very horny,” Carter Sherman, the author of The Second Coming: Sex and the Next Generation's Fight Over Its Future, assured WIRED last year. “They would like to be having sex, and in fact they feel a lot of shame over the fact that they haven't had sex yet or that they’re not having sex enough.” As transitional objects to ease anxieties around physical intimacy, swimsuits and erg machines make a lot of sense. The most salient takeaway from Crew Girl and its ilk might be buried in a less eye-catching finding of 2025’s Teens and Screens, the annual UCLA report on young people’s attitudes toward entertainment media that reliably stokes the “Gen Z hates sex scenes” panic. Relatability is the new watchword. Whereas millennials wallowed in wealth porn, took vicarious flight with superheroes, and sucked the life out of vampire content, today’s adolescents ranked “people with lives like mine” as the No. 1 element they’re looking for in TV shows and movies. Although they’re aspirational enough to feel escapist, with their summer houses and prep-school popularity contests, this season’s teen romances reflect that shift. For a generation raised in the sensory deprivation tank of the internet, whose fears of infinite digital liminality just gave us the horror movies Backrooms and It Ends, the fantasy is humbler than superpowers. It’s being grounded in physical space, a human body capable of sharing tactile experiences with other embodied people. Sex is one way to make those connections. Crew is another.

How The Early Spring Ends—And Sets the Stage for More Chinese Dramas on Netflix
2026-09-14

How The Early Spring Ends—And Sets the Stage for More Chinese Dramas on Netflix

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Shang Zhitao (Sun Qian) and Luan Nian (Jing Boran) in The Early Spring Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A happy ending for Shang Zhitao (Sun Qian) and Luan Nian (Jing Boran) on Netflix’s Chinese workplace romance The Early Spring was incredibly hard-won. At the start of the 24-episode series based on a web novel by Gu Niang Bie Ku, recent college graduate Zhitao is hired by Beijing advertising agency Lime—against bigwig Nian’s wishes. The initial power imbalance between ordinary office worker Zhitao and her boss Nian is significant, and there is a company policy against romantic entanglements between co-workers. Still, they quickly fall into bed together. Over the next six years, the two are on-again, off-again as a prideful, emotionally repressed Nian struggles to say nice things to Zhitao and Zhitao struggles to balance her love for Nian with her commitment to her career. For Zhitao, Beijing isn’t just a place—it is a symbol of her career ambitions, and the kind of life she aspires to. While The Early Spring’s central focus is romance, the C-drama also delves into other aspects of Zhitao’s character and journey. A major throughline of the show is Zhitao’s efforts to make Beijing a home, and to succeed not just at a company or in a bustling metropolis, but in one of the most prestigious companies in one of China’s most global cities. So, when Zhitao chooses to leave Beijing in Episode 21, “A Grand Retreat,” she isn’t just retreating from Nian, she is retreating from the lifestyle that Beijing demands for success. It’s not just one event that informs Zhitao’s decision to leave Beijing. When she tells Nian that she wants to get married and he tells her he doesn’t really think about it, Zhitao begins to reconsider their relationship. Then, one of Zhitao’s best friends dies by suicide. Finally, she gets passed over for a promotion in favor of a less experienced, nepo-baby co-worker. When she seeks comfort from Nian, he offers none. It’s the final straw for Zhitao, who begins to feel like all the time and energy she has committed to their relationship might not have been worth it. Heading into the final installments of the drama, Zhitao has finally called it quits on her toxic dynamic with Nian, moving home to Harbin and starting her own advertising agency in the snowy, northeastern city where she grew up. A few years pass before a bereft Nian attempts to reconcile. Zhitao’s company has partnered with Lime for a local project, and Nian uses it as an excuse to see Zhitao again. Zhitao is understandably reluctant to let Nian back into her life, but Nian uses the excuse of their dog, a Samoyed named Luke, to spend time with his ex-girlfriend. Over the following months, Nian elbows his way into Zhitao’s life and attempts to show her that he has changed. He apologizes for his past behavior, and mostly listens to her when she asks for space. Eventually, Zhitao’s love for Nian wins out and she lets him back into her heart and her life. Nian makes it clear to Zhitao’s parents that he intends to marry their daughter, and then proposes to Zhitao by decorating an art gallery with photos from their time together. He calls it Flora’s Museum of Time and Eternity, using the English name Zhitao used when she worked at Lime. Lian gifts Zhitao a necklace with the “L” key from the typewriter Zhitao knocked over in his office during their first in-person meeting. He invites Zhitao’s closest friends and family, and asks the woman he has loved for almost a decade to marry him. She says yes, finally believing that the two can have a relationship as equals. In the final scenes of the show, we see Zhitao and Nian splitting their time between Harbin and Beijing, happy together. The lovers at the center of The Early Spring Courtesy of Netflix The Early Spring has done incredibly well on Youku and Netflix, where the C-drama has steadily been in the Global Top Ten list. East Asian dramas don’t tend to have second seasons, but The Early Spring could easily be extended with the adaptation of the second book in the web novel series. Titled Miss Talkative, the sequel novel follows the unlikely romance between Zhitao’s work bestie Lumi and her stoic, more traditional co-worker Will. A developing relationship between the two is hinted at in The Early Spring TV series, but the plot line is left unresolved. A scene from the new show's finale Courtesy of Netflix Though Netflix has become known as a home for Korean dramas, with the U.S. streamer investing billions of dollars into the East Asian country’s TV and film industries, the same has not been true for Chinese dramas. China is not interested in giving any U.S. company too much power in its economy, which has kept the streamer from setting up shop in the country. In 2016, Netflix released a letter to shareholders alongside a quarterly earnings report stating: “The regulatory environment for foreign digital content services in China has become challenging. We now plan to license content to existing online service providers in China rather than operate our own service in China in the near term.” That same year, the Chinese government implemented a de facto ban on South Korean entertainment in China following the announcement that the U.S. would be installing an anti-missile system in South Korea. Netflix is still not legally available in China. In the decade since, Netflix’s catalogue and business model have become increasingly international. As Netflix focuses on expansion into international markets, C-dramas have become an increasingly valuable asset. While C-dramas have a growing audience in the U.S., they are even more popular in countries like Pakistan, Bangladesh, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Thailand, Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, and Vietnam. “Audiences in Asia have long loved this category, but we're making them accessible to an even bigger global viewership,” Maya Huang, Head of Chinese-Language Content at Netflix, tells TIME. Netflix has been growing their Mainland China offerings since 2024. Recent hits have included historical fantasy romance Pursuit of Jade and costume drama Ashes to Crown, adding to the organic popularity of earlier series like The Untamed, a historical fantasy romance based on a BL web novel that blew up in popularity during the pandemic. As Netflix looks towards the future, C-dramas are part of their plan, with “a team dedicated to finding the most authentic and best-in-class Chinese language stories” including ones—like The Early Spring—that are simulcast with a Chinese streamer. “With Netflix's reach, dubs and subtitles, and our ability to release titles simultaneously worldwide, we're seeing audiences discover these stories in the U.S., South America, Europe and the Middle East,” says Huang. “We want to give these fans more of what they love—so there's plenty more to come from our Chinese language slate.”

Why You Should Never Wear Shoes in Your House
2026-09-14

Why You Should Never Wear Shoes in Your House

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Every time you welcome a guest into your home, you may be letting in thousands of other ones as well—if you allow your visitors to wear their shoes when they enter. The world outside your door is a messy place—full of bacteria, viruses, and environmental toxins—and the soles of your shoes can pick up a lot. According to one 2023 CBS News/YouGov poll, 63% of Americans regularly remove their shoes when they get home, and 24% ask their guests to do the same. This year, the Indiana Department of Health launched a “Shoes Free Protects You and Me” ad campaign about the benefits of a no-shoes policy at home, targeting tracked-in lead in particular. How serious is the risk that shoes pose, and should your house be a no-shoes zone? The published research on shoe contamination is not extensive, but some stands out. In one 2008 study, Charles Gerba, professor of virology at the University of Arizona, studied 26 pairs of shoes that had been worn for three months in the outside world and came up with some alarming results. The shoes carried between 3,600 and 8 million colony-forming units of bacteria, both on the inside and outside of the shoes. E. coli, or fecal bacteria, was the most common organism being tracked around. “[About] 80% of the bottoms of the shoes had fecal bacteria on them,” Gerba wrote in an email to TIME, “largely picking them up from the floors of public restrooms, which always have fecal bacteria on them. They may also pick them up outside, in places that dogs and birds are.” According to Gerba’s numbers, shoes are significantly more germ-ridden than public toilet seats, which have an average of 3,200 colony forming bacterial units. E. coli is not the only infectious agent that shoes carry around. According to University of Houston researchers, 40% of shoes tested carry C. difficile, an intestinal bacterium that causes diarrhea. Health care settings are particularly germ-filled. One 2016 meta-analysis in the Journal of Applied Microbiology surveyed 13 studies, tracing where shoes have traveled and what they picked up there. A small study in that group found that after physicians did rounds in hospitals, 65% of their shoes tested positive for either antibiotic-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) or Enterococcus faecalis, another gastrointestinal bacterium. Another found that 48% of shoes worn in operating rooms tested positive for at least one pathogenic bacterium, most commonly Staphylococcus. Rural areas, where livestock, birds, and other animals are common, are ready sources of Salmonella and other bacteria. One 2009 study conducted in rural Alaska had people put on sterilized boots, then walk along a randomly selected outdoor path, and finally step onto a piece of sterilized linoleum and remove the boots. From just that walk, 70% of the boots were positive for multiple gut bacteria. People then wore the contaminated boots on short walks across a sterile floor. On half of those walks, bacteria were transferred to the flooring. Urban settings present their own perils, particularly as a result of dogs being walked on sidewalks and in parks. Even in cities where owners routinely pick up after their dogs, traces of fecal bacteria—and pools of urine—regularly collect on heavily foot-trafficked routes. In one 2023 study published in Design, Architecture, and Ergonomics, investigators from Northeastern University in New York City sampled sidewalk rain puddles, shoe bottoms, and flooring inside one of the school’s buildings on its urban campus. On average, they found 30,000 gut bacteria per 100 milliliters of water in puddles on the neighborhood sidewalks. Every shoe sole they sampled from student and teacher volunteers carried varying levels of the same bacteria, and the interior of the building was also contaminated—especially in carpeted areas. “The surface of your flooring makes an enormous difference,” says Alessandra Leri, professor of chemistry and biology at the school and the lead author of the study. “If you have carpets, they function as a reservoir of bacteria.” But no matter the interior floor surface, she says, “shoe soles are a serious vector of transmission of fecal bacteria from the outdoor environment to the indoor environment.” PFAS, the “forever chemicals” so named for how long they linger in the environment and our bodies, are regularly picked up by shoes and used in manufacturing the soles. Lead dust, which can often be stirred up and released into the air during remodeling of older homes and other buildings, can cling to shoes as well. Pesticides, used both on farms and in home gardens, are similarly free-riders on our shoes. In most homes in which people do not remove their shoes upon entering, the area nearest the front door is generally the most contaminated. “We found that the first four steps at the entrance to the household are the germiest floor area in the home,” says Gerba. “We also did tracer studies putting dye and tracer viruses on the bottom of shoes and could see how they left a trail on the floor.” Wherever contaminants spread in the home, they present the greatest danger to the household members who have the most contact with the floor—typically babies and small children who crawl and play on surfaces. Leri follows a no-shoes policy in her home and leaves her shoes by the door when she enters. She does not ask guests to remove their shoes, especially when she hosts parties. “If you are having a cocktail party, you are sensitive to guests feeling like shoes are a part of their ensemble,” she says. But she does roll up the rugs before company arrives, making it easier to clean the floors when the guests are gone. More people—especially younger people—may be taking a harder line, enforcing a universal ban on shoes for anyone in the home. While only 7% of people over 65 and 13% of people ages  45 to 64 require guests to remove shoes before entering, according to the CBS/YouGov poll, 44% of people in the 18 to 29 demographic do. “I find that many people have already adopted that custom,” says Leri. “It is a more hygienic way of living.”

What Is Actually New About the AI Revolution?
2026-09-14

What Is Actually New About the AI Revolution?

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In just a few years, AI has gone from a specialist topic to something everyone has opinions on. It has been praised as miraculous, condemned as dangerous, and debated everywhere from boardrooms to dinner tables. But between these poles of euphoria and dread lies the reality most of us now face: a technology powerful enough to reshape how work gets done, yet still deeply dependent on human judgment. Too often, headlines focus on extremes, but the practical questions are far more grounded. How do we manage this technology? How do we and our teams work alongside it effectively? How do we capture its benefits without compromising our values or our goals? To find useful answers, we have to start by understanding where we are, what has truly changed, and what has not. Artificial intelligence, after all, is not new. It has been with us since at least 1956, when a small group of computer scientists gathered at Dartmouth College to explore a deceptively simple question: Can machines think? Since then, the technology has seen decades of progress and setbacks, so-called AI summers and winters. AI didn’t suddenly appear in the world in the 2020s. But powerful, general-purpose AI systems pushed the technology into mainstream awareness in ways that earlier breakthroughs never did. It’s natural that the reactions have been polarized; rapid change often leaves people unsure what comes next. Today’s systems are powerful and raise new challenges, and we will take those challenges seriously throughout this book. But they are not as alien or unmanageable as they are sometimes made out to be. We already have decades of lessons and frameworks from earlier generations of AI and from other complex technologies, from aviation to automobiles to power plants, all of which can be adapted to this moment. Understanding what’s the same and what’s different about this moment for AI and work, and how we can build on what we already know, is crucial. If you’re new to AI, I will offer you a map of essential concepts so you can navigate confidently. If you’ve been here for a while, I will reframe the challenge, moving the conversation toward leadership and collaboration rather than technical mastery alone. After all, how we choose to work with AI, and who we’ll become in the process, is something we still get to decide. A number of factors have propelled today’s AI from data science laboratories into the center of everyday business conversations. The technology has not only gotten more powerful, but harder to ignore. For decades, AI was used behind the scenes, embedded in models that (for example) predicted customer churn or flagged fraud. Those systems were specialists, usually trained for one narrow task and confined to it. Today’s AI models are generalists. These “foundation models” are vast neural networks trained on oceans of data and capable of being adapted across contexts. The same model that helps a developer write code can also be harnessed to help a marketer write copy or an HR leader write a job description. That versatility is what brought AI out from the back office and into nearly every corner of knowledge work. A second shift is that AI now speaks our language, literally. We no longer need to code or click through rigid menus to interact with AI. We can simply use everyday language, and the responses come back in polished, humanlike prose, making the technology broadly accessible. These systems can also generate new content, including text, images, and beyond—which is why this wave is often called “generative AI.” But that ease of use creates a new kind of business responsibility. We must now learn when to trust AI’s output and when to challenge or shape it. The third change, and perhaps the most profound, is agency. AI no longer just analyzes or predicts. Now it acts. “Agentic AI” can plan steps toward a goal, call for the right tools, check its own work, and keep going from there. A single AI agent can draft an email, open a ticket, schedule a delivery, and log the transaction, all without a human clicking “send.” This can bring immense productivity potential but also raises new questions. How much autonomy should we give AI? What does accountability look like when decisions are distributed across humans and machines? Put these shifts together, and it makes for potentially confusing implications for how work gets done. If AI can “reason” through tasks and carry out steps without a human at every turn (and if its outputs look and sound like a colleague’s message rather than robotic communication) the line can start to blur between using AI as a tool and AI being part of the team. But just like previous waves of AI, generative AI (which creates new content like text, images, or code) and agentic AI (which can take actions independently to complete multistep tasks) are good at some things and bad at others. An AI agent can draft a sophisticated legal memo. But it can also confidently misread a contract’s indemnity clause. It can write working code, yet stumble on a small, unspoken requirement a junior engineer would catch. Harvard’s Fabrizio Dell’Acqua (with his colleagues) describes this as the “jagged technological frontier” of AI. AI can amaze us with its ability to do some tasks and similarly stupefy us with its lack of common sense regarding others. The task for us now is to learn that frontier: to know when to rely on AI, when to supervise it, and when to step in with uniquely human judgment. But to do that well, we need a clearer map of the technology’s actual capabilities and its limitations. Excerpted from Manage the Machine: How to Harness Human-AI Collaboration at Work by Paula Goldman with permission from Basic Venture. Disclosure: Salesforce chair and CEO Marc Benioff is the owner of TIME. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Trump Says He Backs a United Ireland, Stoking a Divisive Debate

Trump Says He Backs a United Ireland, Stoking a Divisive Debate

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Taoiseach Micheal Martin and U.S. President Donald Trump attend a bilateral meeting at Farmleigh House in Dublin, Ireland, on Sept. 12, 2026. U.S. President Donald Trump said Saturday that he would “love to see” a united Ireland and believed it was “going to happen eventually,” breaking with decades of careful neutrality by U.S. presidents over Northern Ireland’s constitutional status. “You have Northern Ireland, and you have Ireland,” Trump told reporters on Sunday while visiting a golf tournament ​at one of his resorts in the Irish village of Doonbeg. “It would seem to me that one of the ‘naturals’ of all time is to put them together.” It’s also not the first time Trump has discussed Northern Ireland and the Irish Republic: he joked about a “merger” of the two in March, though the Northern Ireland official he was addressing at the time, who opposes unification, emphasized that the comment was clearly in jest only. The President seemed aware that his recent remarks would garner backlash, particularly from the British. “The U.K. would have something to say about it obviously, but I think it would be a great thing,” he said Saturday, while in conversation with Irish Taoiseach Micheál Martin. “I mean, how can it be bad?” Trump’s statements come weeks after newly installed U.K. Prime Minister Andy Burnham said there was not enough public support for a referendum on Northern Ireland’s future. Other British politicians have balked at Trump’s statements, arguing that Northern Ireland is indisputably part of the U.K. Martin also downplayed the U.S. leader’s remarks. “It shouldn't be a surprise,” Martin told reporters on Sunday, adding that Trump “takes a different sort of perspective on things.” The Irish leader said that some responses to Trump, however, were an overreaction. “They asked me about Scotland—that one I won't talk about yet,” Trump said Sunday, referencing another country divided on leaving the U.K. The Trump family also owns a golf ​resort in Scotland, which voted against independence from the rest of Great Britain in 2014. “I’ll save that for another day.” The island of Ireland was divided in 1921 following the Irish War of Independence. While a northern region of six counties remained part of the U.K., the rest of the island, comprising 26 counties, became the Irish Free State, which eventually became a sovereign republic in 1949. From the 1960s, tensions in the north between largely Catholic Irish nationalists, or republicans, who wish to reunite with the Republic of Ireland, and mainly Protestant unionists who want to stay with Britain culminated in a three-decade, violent period known as the Troubles. A 1998 peace agreement—dubbed the Good Friday Agreement and mediated in large part by the U.S.—ended the violence and recognized the right of those in Northern Ireland to recognize themselves as British, Irish, or both. It also recognized that Northern Ireland’s status must be based on democratic consent. Following the agreement, Northern Ireland has not yet held a referendum. But Irish nationalism gained momentum when Sinn Féin, the predominant Irish republican party, won the most seats in the Northern Ireland Assembly in 2022. It was the first time a republican party dominated the assembly since Northern Ireland’s formation, though Sinn Féin must still share power with unionists under local laws. Polls, however, show that the Northern Irish public is still divided on whether it should leave the U.K., and Sinn Féin in Northern Ireland has focused on socioeconomic issues over reunification in recent years. In the Republic of Ireland, Sinn Féin is the largest single party, but it belongs to the opposition after two more moderate center-right parties formed the government following the 2024 election, with Martin as the current leader. Some have criticized the Irish government under Martin for its slow uptake of the unification issue. Ireland’s Sinn Féin president Mary Lou McDonald said after Trump’s comments: “The conversation on Irish unity has shifted, and the onus is on the Taoiseach to begin planning.” Claire Hanna, who leads the small nationalist Social Democratic and Labour Party in Northern Ireland, also welcomed Trump’s support. “Turns out he’s not as orange as he looks,” Hanna said, in reference to the political color associated with the Protestant and unionist community in Ireland. The 1998 Good Friday Agreement stipulates that the U.K.’s Northern Ireland secretary can decide to hold a poll and must call one if they believe a majority would vote for Irish reunification. Chris Bryant, the incumbent secretary, posted this section of the agreement on social media amid Trump’s unification remarks, adding: “Just in case anyone should need a quick refresher course on constitutional matters in Northern Ireland.” In a visit to Northern Ireland last month, Burnham said that calling for such a poll is “off the table,” citing the divisiveness of past votes on Brexit and Scottish independence. “We’ve got to focus on bringing people together as best we can,” the British Prime Minister told the BBC. The U.S. President also rankled some on the British far-right who usually side with him. “Northern Ireland is a proud and integral part of the United Kingdom,” Reform UK leader Nigel Farage, a known Trump ally, wrote on social media, explaining that “the people of Northern Ireland neither want a referendum, nor unification.” Rupert Lowe, another British parliamentarian who heads the Restore Britain party, called Trump’s statements “unacceptable” and “insulting,” and suggested that the President “should not be aiming to permanently damage our country to get back at the current malign Labour Government.” Lowe’s claims come as Trump has expressed frustration over the U.K.'s refusal to get involved in the U.S.’s war against Iran. Kemi Badenoch, who leads the opposition Conservative Party, posted on social media that Trump was “shooting from the hip as usual, just like he did with Greenland and Canada becoming the 51st state,” though she argued that “this sort of loose talk about the future of democratic countries is deeply unhelpful and creates unnecessary tension.” The First Ministers of the U.K.’s devolved nations—Wales, Scotland, and Northern Ireland—signed a Memorandum of Understanding Monday, calling for each country to have the “right to determine its own future.” Michelle O'Neill of Sinn Féin joined Scotland's John Swinney of the Scottish National Party (SNP) and Wales' Rhun ap Iorwerth of Plaid Cymru as they insisted no Westminster government has the right to “block democracy or undermine the principle that our people will decide their own future.” “What unites us is a determination to ensure that the voices of our nations are heard, that our people have the opportunity to thrive, and that cooperation between Scotland, Wales, and Ireland can be strengthened," the agreement read. O’Neil wants Ireland to be reunited. Swinney and ap Iorwerth are open to calls for independence from their nations. O’Neil and Swinney urged for referendums in their respective countries, while Rhun ap Iorwerth declined to set a timetable for a Welsh independence vote.

Who Needs Regulation When Trump Is President?
2026-09-14

Who Needs Regulation When Trump Is President?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
One consistent complaint against the Trump administration is that it is imposing personalist rule, in which the whims of the leader override the law. Now Donald Trump has come out forcefully against regulating artificial intelligence—and his argument is based almost explicitly on the superiority of his omniscience over any formal legal process. He is not so much refuting the charge of personalism as turning it into a promise. “The only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” he wrote in a self-refuting post on Truth Social. Even the most market-friendly economist will concede that government intervention is required when any private activity creates an “externality”—that is, a cost imposed on people who are not involved in a transaction. A factory dumping toxic waste into a stream has heretofore provided the textbook case of an externality necessitating regulation. But AI bots destroying human civilization—as executives are warning could happen—clearly represents a much costlier externality. This is, in fact, probably the strongest conceivable case for regulation. The intense competitive pressure among AI firms, which have a much stronger incentive to innovate quickly than to ensure their innovations are safe, enhances the case further. Skeptics have valid reasons to worry about poorly designed regulation that either entrenches the current industry leaders or permits Chinese AI to overtake the American version. Those are reasons to demand well-designed regulations, not to avoid any rules at all. Read: The singularity is not what it seems Against this overwhelming rationale, Trump offers, in place of any neutral standards, his characteristic faith in his own prerogatives. Trump tends to discount the need for laws even in areas where he favors government action. He opposed congressional authorization for war with Iran. He has not asked Congress to levy tariffs, preferring instead to raise or lower them and provide carve-outs to affected firms based on his whim. If anyone needs it, the incumbent president’s behavior provides excellent evidence against personalist rule in general and, in the case of AI, specifically. For one thing, his decisions are not necessarily aligned with the public interest. Trump has formed a political alliance with a handful of wealthy men who have a strong personal stake in the development and shape of AI. The dual roles of David Sacks, Trump’s most important AI adviser and an investor in AI, have caused even some White House officials to express concern about conflicts of interest. Additionally, Trump has a long-standing tendency to focus on short-term concerns over long-term ones. Trump’s preference for immediate gratification explains many of his decisions, including defunding scientific and medical research, accelerating fossil-fuel emissions, and denigrating American soft power globally. Pushing forward AI development delivers financial benefits to Trump allies who have plied his party with donations. Trump seems to believe that the AI build-out is driving the economy at the moment. It is difficult to imagine any long-term concern persuading Trump to forgo the short-term benefits of the AI boom. Finally, on the IQ point, Trump is correct that a high level of analytic intelligence is necessary to regulate wisely. This is not, however, a reason to empower Donald Trump. Although he possesses a distinct animal cunning and an uncanny sense for human weakness, Trump isn’t the type to dig in and understand new problems in all of their complexity. He does not appear to read texts of any length or substance, and he is reportedly unable to tolerate briefings of any depth. His public remarks are rambling and error-filled, and rely on a shrinking pool of vocabulary terms. Trump does have expertise in a handful of realms, such as construction, entertainment, and men’s discount shoeware. Artificial intelligence and technology in general are not among his fields of specialization. There are many reasons the United States needs clear, stable regulations on artificial intelligence, rather than relying on the whims of a leader. That is true even if the leader is public-minded and deeply versed in policy intricacies. It is all the more true if the leader is Donald Trump.

Why Does Donald Trump Hate This Sculpture?
2026-09-14

Why Does Donald Trump Hate This Sculpture?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
The sculpture that Donald Trump wants removed from the National Mall has a reputation even among its admirers for being a bit inscrutable. Dedicated in 1967 at what is now the National Museum of American History, José de Rivera’s Infinity looks, fittingly, like an infinity symbol with an extra twist. (A bow? A pretzel?) By pairing the technical prowess required to create smooth, curved metal with the delicate spinning of a music box, the slow-turning work seems to take a different form each time you pass it. The museum’s architect, Walker O. Cain, wrote that Infinity “has the characteristic of stimulating everyone who has seen it to create his own personal imaginative interpretation.” But the president does not appear to have been stimulated. “That strange modernist sculpture tells visitors nothing about America, inspires no one, and honors none of our Great American Heroes,” Trump wrote on Truth Social Friday night. Trump insisted that the Smithsonian replace Infinity with a 30-foot high “Colossus” of George Washington. In the same post, he called for the mounting of a five-year exhibition about the Founding Father to open immediately (museum shows of such magnitude typically take years to plan), and said the museum should create a “Center for American Heroes.” Trump also wrote that the institution should add another, 11-foot-tall Washington statue to the museum’s Flag Hall. That one was displayed at the Grand Prix race held in Washington, D.C., this summer as part of Trump’s Freedom 250 festivities. He’d like it dedicated at the museum this Thursday. Trump’s latest demands might be partially understood as a test of how far he can push an institution that he does not control. They came just three days after the Smithsonian’s secretary, Lonnie Bunch, announced his upcoming retirement, following a year and a half of standing between the museum complex and the White House. In a July executive order, the president called on the National Park Service, which is part of the executive branch, to place signs outside the National Museum of American History accusing it of partisan bias. Few would question the appropriateness of having a healthy amount of George Washington in an American-history museum, but Trump’s ask might be a little too redundant. For one thing, a Washington sculpture can already be found on the museum’s second floor. Classical in style and portraying the first president as a kind of Zeus figure, Horatio Greenough’s 1841 statue boasts chiseled muscles that rival even the machismo AI depictions Trump circulates of himself and Washington online. And when approached from the east, Rivera’s Infinity—the aspirational site of Trump’s Washington colossus—elegantly frames another abstract form: the obelisk known as the Washington Monument. Trump’s call for changes to the museum fits a few recent patterns: He has been on a campaign to institute his preferred version of history in the national museum system. He has begun to leave his mark, and occasionally his name, across the architectural landscape of Washington, D.C. And while doing so, he appears to have indicated a distaste for abstract art. Earlier this month, the Kennedy Center, which Trump controls, took down Joel Shapiro’s playful metal sculpture Blue. When the Trump administration—addressing a controversy at the National Portrait Gallery last year—criticized a decidedly representational Amy Sherald painting of a trans woman as Lady Liberty, part of its complaint was that, in the words of the administration, “the Statue of Liberty is not an abstract canvas for political expression.” The use of abstract reads like an insult. Trump has pushed a classical makeover in D.C.. He has issued executive orders mandating classical designs as the default for new federal buildings, for example, and remounted figurative statues that were torn down during the Black Lives Matter movement. He has mused about the White House upgrading its columns to a higher Greek order (adding more classical to the neoclassical); his most prized project, the White House ballroom, looks like a Corinthian-columned department store. In his first executive order on architecture, he said federal buildings should command respect. When it comes to the Rivera outside the National Museum of American History, Trump’s issue with it—and with abstraction largely—might not be that the sculpture “says nothing about America,” but that it doesn’t say what he wants it to. That is, he can’t control what it says at all. The American artist Robert Motherwell wrote in a 1951 essay celebrating abstraction that one of the most striking aspects of the approach is its “nakedness,” or the way it turns, with such decisiveness, away from the symbols we know. “How many rejections on the part of her artists! Whole worlds—the world of objects, the world of power and propaganda, the world of anecdotes, the world of fetishes and ancestor worship,” he wrote. With abstract works such as Infinity, we’re free to find our own reading—or not. Whereas abstraction attempts to reject passed-down meanings, the image of George Washington is layered with them. Part of what is so moving about, say, getting up close to Washington’s military uniform at the National Museum of American History is the way it challenges how we’ve come to see him as an icon rather than as an actual person. Read: A strikingly complex portrait of a Founding Father Abstraction has long been subject to right-wing attacks. During the Red Scare, for instance, George A. Dondero, a U.S. representative from Michigan, crusaded to rid American art of what he saw as communist influence. In a 1949 speech that could be ripped from Truth Social today, he framed a list of 20th-century art movements as un-American weapons for destruction: “Dadaism aims to destroy by ridicule. Expressionism aims to destroy by aping the primitive and insane,” he said. “Abstractionism aims to destroy by the creation of brainstorms.” Sure, the unpredictable responses to abstract art—the brainstorms—can be a little intimidating. How many of us have stood, a bit clueless, before Jackson Pollock’s riotous colors or Cy Twombly’s frenetic scratches and wondered if we might need a Ph.D. for this? But there’s also a certain joy to find in it. One National Museum of American History curator, David Shayt, embraced the compounding, shifting meanings of abstraction in a 2008 essay about the museum’s Rivera. The sculpture marked an early work of abstract art commissioned by the U.S. government, but, Shayt argues, the commission was more about the government’s belief that Rivera, a prominent abstract-expressionist sculptor known for his twisting metal forms, could capture the museum’s dual identity in its early days as the National Museum of History and Technology. Cain, the architect, had convinced the government that the sculpture would be a mechanical model of the solar system, a “twenty-first-century orrery.” But Rivera, who cited the abstractionist Piet Mondrian and the sculptor Constantin Brâncuși as among his influences, asserted no such thing. “What I make represents nothing but itself,” he said. That didn’t stop the National Museum of American History’s staff from making their own meaning over the years, particularly as employees of a “pragmatic museum of history rather than a museum of art,” Shayt wrote. “Is it a mathematical symbol? A wisp of battlefield smoke? A tennis stroke?” he asked of Infinity. One of the sculpture’s greatest attributes, he continued, is “an expressiveness that can evolve in step with the evolution of the museum whose entrance it fronts.” For an example of the kind of aesthetic Trump prefers, you don’t have to go far from the Rivera. A short walk brings you to Freedom Plaza, where the National Park Service has installed a pop-up exhibition for the nation’s 250th birthday. Rows of traditional, metal statues of Revolutionary War soldiers line the plaza—many, notably, represent enslaved individuals. Standing on a high plinth, meanwhile, is Caesar Rodney, a slaveholder and Declaration of Independence signer, who looms over the display. The statue had been removed in Delaware during the Black Lives Matter protests, only to be restored in Washington as some conservatives began championing him as a symbol of the left’s overreach. Trump has placed a similar style of traditional statue—direct, simple—throughout the White House Rose Garden and envisions much of the same for the Garden of American Heroes, another of his planned D.C. projects, where abstraction is explicitly forbidden. There’s very little room for interpretation in these sorts of statues. They mean what they mean. Much of the Trump-era fight over history has boiled down to a struggle about trust. The Trump administration says the history the Smithsonian tells is not patriotic enough; the institution counters that Americans can hold patriotism and complexity at once. The National Museum of American History museum’s director, Anthea Hartig, told a congressional committee this summer: “Telling our history fully is not anti-American. It is a form of respect for the American people.”  Abstraction, too, holds a kind of esteem for its viewers, trusting them to navigate its symbol-less terrain. In his essay, Motherwell wrote that the emergence of abstract art is one sign that there are still people who “know how to respect and follow their inner feelings, no matter how irrational or absurd they may first appear.” On the west side of the museum stands another abstract piece, a massive, 40-foot Alexander Calder that the museum calls a “monument to modernity.” Its jagged black edges reach outward, conveying a sense of rupture or change. On Saturday, the area around the Calder was roped off with caution tape, surely something to do with conservation or work on the museum grounds. But it felt a little ominous, as though it were a warning not for museumgoers, but for the art itself.

A Nation of Strange, Idle Men
2026-09-14

A Nation of Strange, Idle Men

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Like many Millennial men, I used to be pretty invested in Ye, the artist formerly known as Kanye West—primarily because of his music, but also because of his unique position as the one celebrity who seemed empowered to behave however he wanted. He interrupted Taylor Swift at the Video Music Awards; he said “George Bush doesn’t care about Black people” at a Hurricane Katrina fundraiser; he brought a Jesus look-alike onstage. I would shrug, and keep listening. Then his do what I want ethos went very bad. Ye called slavery “a choice”; he came out as a fan of Hitler; he wore a KKK-style hood. With escalating horror, I questioned what had happened. Was it about having too much money? Untreated mental illness? I wondered when logic and shame might kick in—when someone might get him to stop. But the more he was rejected by his peers, the media, and some fans, the more Kanye seemed to act out. Eventually, I accepted, grimly, that I was watching someone who, feeling abandoned and misunderstood, was determined to ignore all of his critics and double down on pursuing his chosen path. I thought about Ye while I was reading The Castle, a new nonfiction work by the journalist Jon Ronson that attempts to unpack the motivations of wayward men who no longer fit into traditional society. Ronson is probably best-known for his 2015 book, So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed, a journalistic account of the emotional incentives and historical context behind the social-media-fueled frenzy that became known as “cancel culture.” Like the people in that book, many of the subjects of The Castle have been shoved to the margins. But the forces at play in this new book are more disparate, and perhaps more stubborn, than an angry online mob. Ronson explores how men become alienated because they can’t achieve success in their chosen field, because they can’t pay their bills, because they have too much money, because the town around them went to hell. The book shows how their free will, coupled with a lot of free time on the internet, leads them to pursue their self-interest as best they can—which, eventually, begets more alienation. The Castle opens with the story of a strange encounter with a strange man. One night in 2023, Ronson’s 20-something son, Joel, and three of his friends (two are women) are invited to a mysterious castle in the middle of nowhere—specifically, Woodstock, Connecticut—for what they believe is a party. There, the women are asked to participate in a bizarre dating show hosted by a man named Chris Mark. The idea is apparently that 101 women will dress up like princesses and vie to be Mark’s girlfriend; once the situation becomes predictably awkward, Mark seemingly deems the night a bust and kicks everyone out. Ronson, who wakes up to a slew of texts from Joel describing what happened, is alarmed by the whole episode—and decides he needs to meet Mark himself. On their first meeting, Mark at first seems transparent about how he spends his time. He owns the castle, where he lives with several women whom he met online. The entire scene strikes Ronson as reminiscent of Hugh Hefner’s grotto—an analogy they discuss at Ronson’s prompting. But Mark, in Ronson’s telling, quickly grows antagonistic, abruptly asking Ronson to sign an NDA, then later barraging him with suspicious texts and threatening him with a pair of goons. Ronson concludes that Mark has “nurtured a private fantasy of manhood” that the journalist has put at risk by asking questions. (Mark has contested many of the details and claims of The Castle, including that he sent anyone to threaten Ronson, and has said that Ronson “presented me and events involving me in a profoundly misleading manner.”) The Castle is about both Ronson’s investigation into Mark and the bigger phenomenon Mark seems to represent: too-idle men, most of them less wealthy than Mark, littering the country, yearning for some kind of grander purpose. He’s especially interested in men ages 25 to 54 who are both unemployed and no longer looking for work—people he claims have “opted out of society,” choosing instead a “life of male nothingness.” In a moment of general anxiety about men—whom they vote for, what they’re reading, whether they share their feelings—his interest isn’t political. It’s empathic. He offers no prescriptive solutions, wags no fingers. Instead, he wants the reader to conceive of the scale of social anomie, and to envision the millions of other men chasing meaning in the wreckage of their former aspirations. In particular, Ronson focuses on a demographic whose life expectancy has dropped in the 21st century: middle-aged white men without college degrees, many of whom suffer from what a pair of Princeton economists he speaks with define as “deaths of despair” brought on by “the erosion of a way of life.” Once upon a time, such men—who widely supported Donald Trump in all three of his runs for president—were sold on the American dream. Now some of them are characterized by “a kind of cowed, vaguely shocked hopelessness,” Ronson writes. Others, facing social rejection, have started to live “like separatists, creating worlds in which they didn’t have to live or interact with other people except on their own terms.” Women, too, suffer from deaths of despair—but it’s the men, Ronson indicates, who have been raised to envision themselves as heroes, a self-image that can drive them to dark places. Ronson illustrates how easy it can be for these men to slide off the map. His most endearing subject is Greg Chism, a father of two daughters in small-town Illinois. Chism was a single parent with tons of medical debt living in a place without a lot of job opportunities when, in the 2010s, he discovered a YouTube channel encouraging viewers to “make a video and change your life.” A few of his early videos got some attention, after which Chism received coaching from the platform’s employees, and he eventually struck on a winning formula: bizarre sketches involving his two daughters that played like the output of a hallucinating LLM fed on Nickelodeon. These generated millions of views, and some of them got really weird; Chism and his kids dressed like babies, or pretended to eat cat food off pizza. Soon came the blowback: Chism was, the internet claimed, a bad father abusing his children by making them participate in these creepy videos. His channel was demonetized, then banned. He was shunned just as quickly as he’d been celebrated. Read: The real reason men should read fiction But Chism, whom Ronson tracks down, has not been radicalized by the wave of hatred that came his way. Nor does he seem to be a bad father; his (now teenage) kids express mostly fond memories of their channel. He admits making errors in judgment—see: cat food—but in the end, Ronson writes, Chism was “a single father juggling responsibilities while feeling foolishly idealistic about sharing his family’s lives on the internet.” Becoming a YouTuber had seemed like a way for Chism to stop sliding toward destitution. Instead, his entire life was put under a microscope. Ronson uses Chism’s guilelessness to show how unexpected and profound the pitfalls can be for anyone looking to find a foothold in the new world. So You’ve Been Publicly Shamed examined a group of people whose lives were ruined by the internet in one particular way; The Castle flips the framing. In the latter book’s view, the internet allows its users to choose their own particular off-ramp from ennui. Seeking female companionship, Chris Mark turns to dating apps; eager to feel seen, Greg Chism becomes a YouTuber. One subject, the podcaster Andrew Gold, begins tailoring his content to anti-woke audiences, racking up huge numbers by playing to the preexisting grievances of his angry followers. Another chapter digs into Ronson’s rocky friendship with the comedy writer Graham Linehan, whose involvement in the gender-critical movement led him to act out online in ways that Ronson reads as self-destructive. (Linehan has disputed many of the details of Ronson’s depiction, and called it “an indictment of him and the cowardly, careerist, mendacious class of people who could roughly gather under the title ‘Graham Linehan’s old friends and colleagues.’”) A swerve comes toward the end when Ronson wonders whether he has fallen into the same trap as some of those men. By treating Mark’s story as something only he can make sense of, Ronson has neglected his friends and his marriage. “Everyone I met for this book had, I thought, a similarly dysfunctional relationship with purpose, hunting too desperately for it,” he writes. “And now there was a generation of men, unanchored from society and going it alone—as content creators, culture war influencers, amateur sleuths.” Ronson doesn’t build toward any prescriptive conclusions; he gives these men a platform to explain how they feel, and allows the reader to weigh the validity of those feelings. I remember the Chism pile-on well, and was slightly horrified to hear the story from his perspective—how confusing it must have felt to toggle so rapidly and randomly between being seen as heroic and villainous. Ronson’s empathy is not boundless; he draws hard lines around some of Mark’s behavior. But he seems to understand that his personal judgment is more or less meaningless. The factors shaping these men are external, but how they react comes from the inside—and many of them remain trapped in their own metaphorical castles, far from the light. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Americans Don’t Understand Who’s Rich
2026-09-14

Americans Don’t Understand Who’s Rich

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Terry Taylor is worth about $2 billion, and you have almost surely never heard of him. Based in Florida, he doesn’t put his name on the car dealerships that he buys up, and he reportedly attends conferences only on the condition that no one announces who he is. Taylor came to our attention in 2023, when he paid $76 million for an Aspen mansion—then the most expensive home sale in the Colorado town’s history. In 2024, Forbes magazine, which keeps tabs on the richest people in the United States and around the world, slotted Taylor in at No. 1,694 on its World Billionaires List and published a profile titled “The Car Dealership Billionaire No One Knows.” “He’s like Batman,” a source told Forbes. “You hear about him and you admire his work, but nobody has really ever seen him in real life.” When Americans picture the ultrarich, they typically think of tech billionaires such as Elon Musk and Mark Zuckerberg, whose wealth lies predominantly in shares of publicly traded companies. They might also think of Wall Street financiers and celebrities such as Taylor Swift. But far more typical are figures like Terry Taylor—which is to say, owners of successful privately held businesses. We are economists who have been studying taxation and inequality for more than a decade. By our calculations, about 1.7 million Americans have each built a net worth of at least $10 million by owning a private business. For every CEO of a public company, there are more than 1,000 private-business owners with a net worth of more than $25 million. In the course of our research, we’ve come to call these people “everywhere millionaires” because they exist all around the country, in a huge range of industries. Perhaps not surprising, some of these millionaires have stakes in investment firms or medical, dental, or legal practices. But the ranks of everywhere millionaires also include a repo man from Henderson, Nevada; a car-wash owner from Milwaukee; a treated-lumber magnate from Alabama; a boat-cover maker from the Ozarks. What we found changed how we think about inequality. During the Occupy Wall Street protests after the 2008 financial crisis, the prototypical one-percenters were executives at big banks. Today, when Senators Elizabeth Warren and Bernie Sanders propose making the rich pay their fair share, they aim at Wall Street and Silicon Valley. But far more relevant to the story of inequality in America are the everywhere millionaires who quietly press their elected representatives for favorable treatment. The tax code bears their imprint far more than it does Musk’s. Last year’s One Big Beautiful Bill Act, for example, gutted the electric-vehicle and clean-energy credits, to the Tesla CEO’s detriment. The same bill made permanent a 20 percent deduction that effectively lowers the top tax rate for private-business owners from 37 percent to 30 percent. And it preserved a loophole that lets business owners in most of the country, but not salaried employees, deduct their state and local taxes in full. When asked on Meet the Press why the cut for the highest earners had to be permanent, House Speaker Mike Johnson said: “This is not giving tax cuts to millionaires; it’s the opposite. The people in the tax bracket that you’re referring to, many of them are small-business owners.” Although many top-bracket taxpayers do indeed own businesses, the benefits of these provisions flow overwhelmingly to the wealthiest among them. A loophole that lets private-business owners avoid Medicare taxes has similarly been justified as a break for the little guy. Preserving the family farm has been a pretext for passing ever larger exemptions to the estate tax, to the point that a married couple can now pass on $30 million to heirs tax-free. Hiding behind small business, in short, has proved a devastatingly effective strategy for the rich. The financial affairs of some of the wealthiest Americans have long been opaque. The Forbes 400 began in 1982 at the direction of the publisher Malcolm Forbes, who reportedly had been inspired by the ballroom capacity at the New York socialite Caroline Astor’s Fifth Avenue mansion. At the time, editorial staffers doubted they could find the 400 richest people, let alone measure their wealth. Although for public companies, people who own a large number of shares must disclose their holdings, and the compensation of top executives is a matter of public record, many wealthy owners of privately held businesses closely guard details about their finances. Kerry Dolan, a Forbes editor, told The New York Times in 2022 that the magazine’s search for billionaires might never pick up on someone who had quietly made $250 million from selling a stake in a business and had invested the proceeds well. The U.S. government’s view into this world is similarly limited. The Federal Reserve’s Survey of Consumer Finances includes a supplemental sample of a few hundred ultrarich respondents. Tracking down even this small number of people requires an expert staff to conduct interviews and a budget of tens of millions of dollars, a survey official told us. Only one in 10 who are contacted cooperates with the survey. The IRS collects voluminous confidential information about businesses and their owners. But it’s stored in a labyrinth of separate systems and databases, making it hard for anyone to use it to answer even basic questions: How many private-business owners are there across the United States? How rich are they, and how did they become rich? Without detailed information on how privately held businesses operate and make money, experts cannot estimate the effects of tax changes and other policies. After the two of us finished graduate school in 2014, we worked as unpaid analysts for the U.S. Treasury’s Office of Tax Analysis. Along with our friend Danny Yagan, we were assigned a project to help the department figure out how much tax various categories of business owners were paying, and we were given a database of anonymized records to analyze. (For research purposes, each taxpayer is assigned a unique identification number. The names and actual tax IDs of the businesses and individuals who submit tax forms are confidential; the IRS cannot legally disclose them even to other parts of the government.) By combining various data sets over the course of months, we could fill out a detailed map of American enterprise and observe how business owners build their fortunes. Even without knowing owners’ names, we learned a lot: We were able to cross-reference the tax data with anonymized data sets from past research containing standardized-test scores, parental income, and early-career trajectories. This information became the statistical foundation for a series of research papers. The more we came to recognize the importance of everywhere millionaires, the more we wanted to understand their decision making. So we sought to identify some through public sources and tell their stories. We looked, for instance, at trade publications that ranked top earners. We interviewed industry experts and combed through databases such as Dun & Bradstreet, which collects reams of information about businesses and their leaders. We also tapped our own networks. The more people we told about our quest to find rich private-business owners, the more leads we gathered. A family member from St. Louis pointed us to the Tracy family, who owns Dot Foods—the nation’s largest food redistributor, which generates more than $10 billion in revenue a year and was started from the back of a station wagon by a dairy farmer and his wife, Dorothy, in rural Illinois. Some of the most memorable businesses we came across were the least glamorous. One was a regional distributor of garbage trucks, street sweepers, and sewer suckers for municipalities. Once we identified the top earners in an industry, we learned more about them through public information, such as newspaper and magazine profiles and published memoirs. Property records for luxury homes and registries of private-jet and yacht owners pointed us to colorful stories. We traced a 100-foot yacht moored off Manhattan Beach to the founder of Bird Barrier, a company that keeps pigeons off buildings. A 383-foot yacht belonged to the man who built Harbor Freight Tools, the discount-tool chain. Others were owned by a forklift manufacturer in New Bremen, Ohio; a Long Island scrap-metal recycler; and a slew of auto dealers. Whatever they sell—cars, treated lumber, boat covers—the everywhere millionaires have one thing in common: They own what have become known as pass-through businesses because their profits skip the traditional corporate tax entirely and flow straight to owners, to be taxed at the individual rate. This structure surged in popularity following the Tax Reform Act of 1986, which cut the top individual tax rate to less than the corporate tax rate for the first time in U.S. history. In the decades since, lawmakers have carved out multiple loopholes, so a dollar earned from owning a business is routinely taxed much less than a dollar earned in wages. Moreover, business owners have a far greater ability to underreport their income to the IRS than wealthy CEOs do. In some sense, getting rich by starting a business is an old American story, but the rise of education-dependent knowledge industries has gotten much more attention in the past few decades. When we were graduating from college, we were drawn to what many American middle-class families have come to see as the conventional paths to prosperity: tech and finance. Eric worked for a time as a software developer in a few start-ups, and Owen as an analyst at a venture-capital firm. But our subsequent economic research showed that studying, going to good schools, and then landing a job working at a prestigious company was hardly the only way to get ahead. Annie Lowrey: The last Americans really paying taxes In the upper reaches of the wealth distribution, the advantages of entrepreneurship appear stark. According to our analysis of the 2022 Survey of Consumer Finances, the 65,000 American households worth more than $100 million consisted almost entirely of business owners; fewer than half held graduate degrees. When we matched entrepreneurs with their test scores, we found that people with average SAT results were more likely to build a highly successful business than to make a comparable fortune as a salaried worker. Owning, it turns out, often beats earning. But for all the benefits of entrepreneurial risk-taking, everywhere millionaires are also the beneficiaries of U.S. government policy, which they shape to their advantage. As Congress drafted the 2017 tax law—which ultimately became the biggest business-tax cut in history—we met an auto dealer who had become a member of Congress and warned that the bill would devastate his industry. Dealers borrow heavily to keep cars on the lot. To help offset the tax cuts, the proposed bill limited how much interest businesses could deduct. By the time the bill passed, though, auto dealers had won a special exemption from that limit. Once you start watching for favors like this, you see these business owners’ influence everywhere: in tax legislation, in market regulation (or the lack thereof), and in the backgrounds of candidates for elected office. Everywhere millionaires don’t get the same attention as eccentric tech titans, finance executives, or famous entertainers. But seeing them is the first step to understanding the economy and society as they really are.

The Atlantic’s October Cover: Anne Applebaum on the “Empire of Grift” and the Revolution in American Foreign Policy
2026-09-14

The Atlantic’s October Cover: Anne Applebaum on the “Empire of Grift” and the Revolution in American Foreign Policy

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
In The Atlantic’s October cover story, “Empire of Grift,” staff writer Anne Applebaum examines how far-right ideologues have transformed American foreign policy into a tool of private gain and antidemocratic reaction. Instead of making the world safe for democracy, she writes, U.S. foreign policy is rewiring the international order. Reporting from Brazil, Applebaum reveals that the United States is not the ally Brazilians once knew. No longer is American power deployed solely by trained diplomats and reliable officials, working through state channels; instead, “informal envoys, private companies, online grifters, and hardened ideologues both inside and outside the government matter more. Unlike their predecessors, these people do not talk about spreading democracy, and don’t want to build long-term relationships. They don’t care about America’s reputation. They are deploying the tools of American diplomacy and government for their own purposes, to promote their own business interests or their own ideological goals, because, suddenly, they can.” Though the Trumpists currently in charge of the U.S. federal government are frequently described as “isolationists,” Applebaum argues that that characterization is wrong. Many of these officials “don’t think of themselves as ordinary Republicans who won an election and will temporarily hold power until the next election. Nor are they mere appendages of Trump. They believe they represent something much greater: the vanguard of a movement that will alter not just America, but the world.” Applebaum continues, “Like earlier revolutionaries, they do not believe that their opponents are legitimate rivals … Their enemies are existential, and they are international: super-spreaders of a ‘wokeness’ plague, or proponents of a fictitious ‘censorship-industrial complex,’ or advocates of ‘regulation’ that will hamper American business, or self-hating promoters of ‘mass migration’ who want to drown Western civilization in a sea of brown-faced aliens. To maintain their power and defeat these enemies, the predators, like their Bolshevik counterparts, cannot rely on traditional diplomacy, because they don’t trust its practitioners.” The democratic world that came into being after 1945—a world based not exclusively on law, but on a set of mostly self-imposed restraints—is changing, Applebaum writes. In the post-1945 democratic order, Applebaum observes, “American power was largely derived from alliances that it refrained from exploiting. Long-term relationships were held to be valuable, because you never knew when you might need your allies.” That U.S.-led democratic order was of enormous benefit to this country and to the world: “America helped build eight decades of peace, prosperity, and eventually democracy in Europe and much of Asia, creating markets for U.S. companies and unprecedented support for U.S. power. This world is fast disappearing. Instead, following the president’s lead, different people inside and outside the administration are seizing the revolutionary moment in order to win zero-sum victories. Countries that hang back from this free-for-all know they will suffer. ‘If we’re not at the table, we’re on the menu,’ Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said at Davos earlier this year. He was right.” Anne Applebaum’s “Empire of Grift” was published today at TheAtlantic.com. Please reach out with any questions or requests. Press contacts: Anna Bross and Paul Jackson | The Atlantic [email protected]
