George Lucas’s Next Great Epic Is an Art Museum
2026-09-14

George Lucas’s Next Great Epic Is an Art Museum

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
What’s your story? The story that you live by, that your cells are geared to, even or especially when you’re not thinking about it? Do we create our own meaning, or should we be trying to discover the meaning that created us? Is it the tractor beams of heavenly love that hold us here, like God’s guy ropes, or are we adrift? Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. When George Lucas, a long time ago in a galaxy far, far away, began writing the screenplay for Star Wars, he had a copy of Joseph Campbell’s The Hero With a Thousand Faces clamped, so to speak, between his teeth. For Campbell, a professor with a popular touch, life was nothing more or less than a summons to greatness. Across cultures, and across the history of the human imagination, he had discerned it, “the one, shape-shifting yet marvelously constant story”: the hero’s journey. Departure, initiation, return. Living is a heroic business, Campbell insisted. Stacked with trials, sure, and frightful reversals, but with the grail floating always ahead of us, calling us onward. Use the Force, Luke. Let go! And now Lucas has built a temple to story. The Lucas Museum of Narrative Art, which opens this month in Exposition Park, just south of downtown Los Angeles, is a swooping, brain-beggaring, super-sexy, twin-bodied leviathan of a megastructure, white as the gown of Princess Leia, half spaceship, half cetacean, all curves, Shangri-la for skateboarders if they could ever get inside. Co-founded by Lucas and his wife, Mellody Hobson, it hoists itself into twinkling California emptiness on an 11-acre site (formerly two parking lots) next to the Natural History Museum and the L.A. Memorial Coliseum. Walking toward it, you think: Jesus. Eighth wonder? Squillion-dollar folly? Architectural embodiment of some impulse from spiritus mundi? Entering it, you stop thinking altogether. As I passed from the dreamy light of the covered forecourt, a single lens-like portal in its ceiling (for maximum diffraction of heavenly tractor beams), and into the space-gobbling atrium, I was conscious only of a delighted vertigo. Like, awe. Like, wow. It was an old-school encounter with the sublime, not unlike the one I’d had as a 9-year-old, in 1977, in a cinema in Cambridge, England, watching the first scene of Star Wars. You remember the first scene of Star Wars. A tiny fleeing Rebel ship zips past us into the star-pricked abyss, laser bolts pinging around it, and then, and then, Darth Vader’s Star Destroyer drones overhead, shears off the top of the screen, the viewer’s eye hauled powerless and silently wow-ing for what feels like miles along the faceted grayness of its Imperial underbelly. Agog in my cinema seat, I was a tiny, grubby Shelley, and before me was Mont Blanc. Lucas and Hobson thought about building their museum in San Francisco; there were prolonged negotiations to build it in Chicago; but it’s landed in the right place: fictive L.A., the Kingdom of Story, where even now, in coffee shops and one-room apartments across the city, on laptops sweating with hope, at least 400 screenplays are being written in accordance with the immemorial template mapped out by Campbell and industrialized by Lucas. Look around: Everyone here is on their hero’s journey. So in Exposition Park it sublimely squats, the Lucas Museum, flaring its contours into the L.A. void. Why is it so bloody enormous? Because it has to be. Because larger and grander and deeper than the campus itself—with its library, two movie theaters, 100,000 square feet of gallery space, and sculpted stretch of public parkland—is the wobbling master-idea of Lucas himself: a museum of narrative art. Of images that tell or contain or project or smell like a story. Which means … pretty much everything in the history of art that isn’t abstract. Which means the story of art itself, from the hovering aurochs and Paleolithic handprints of the cave paintings of Altamira (presented here in a glowing full-size reproduction) to the gods-and-monsters heavy-metal dream time of Paul Raymond Gregory. Which means comics, murals, film, Conan the Barbarian and Frida Kahlo, Andrea Solario’s Salome With the Head of John the Baptist and Robert Crumb’s Book of Genesis. (First frame: God holding a big old hairy ball of Chaos in his hands.) Which means Richard Ansdell’s The Death of Sir William Lambton at the Battle of Marston Moor next to a reproduction of Gustave Courbet’s A Burial at Ornans. Both paintings are frieze-like in structure: In the former, the body of an expired or expiring nobleman is conveyed through a turmoil of cattle, dogs, sheep, and milling cavalry; in the latter, a gang of grim-faced locals assembles round an open grave, down at the village cemetery. In both paintings, death is a kind of antimatter, churning the scene. Am I making it sound random? It’s not random. Some of the galleries are organized around the foundational myths of American culture—work, family, sports—while others are dedicated to a single artist (Maxfield Parrish, for example). It is, however, wildly democratic. Star Wars, as movie geeks know, is a brilliant hodgepodge of influences: Buck Rogers, Kurosawa, The Dam Busters, high culture, trash culture, the lot. At the Lucas Museum, this is part of the character of the collection—everything that tweaked, tickled, or ran its buzzing lightsaber through the imagination of young George Lucas is represented here. The galleries dedicated to comics and cartoons have a particular energy, pulp art exploding off the walls. Downstairs, meanwhile, on giant screens, are giddyingly depthful high-res photographic re-creations of the Sistine Chapel and, nearby, the Hagia Sophia. The effect is slightly Vegas, that throb of alien vividness, that feeling of signs coming loose from their moorings, the Sphinx gazing blandly across Las Vegas Boulevard—but I love Vegas, and America has taught me to love that feeling. Lucas is also a major collector of that singular populist and freakish master of compacted visual storytelling, Norman Rockwell. I was delighted to be able to stand in front of The Connoisseur—which features a well-dressed older gentleman, seen from the rear (Rockwell loved his rears), his body somewhat torqued with interior tension, standing in front of a large Jackson Pollock–style cosmic splatter. Doing so, I participated, as it were, in its sight gag: a man looking at a painting of a man looking at a painting. Very Rockwellian. The amplitude of all of this is not for everybody. Writing in the Los Angeles Times in 2017, after it had been announced that the museum was coming to L.A., the critic Christopher Knight protested that narrative art was a “made-up category.” He scorned the definition then on the museum’s website—“Narrative art is visual art that tells a story”—as a “perfect tautology.” Linguistically, he was quite correct, but then again, I’m not sure how you could fail to have a good time at the Lucas Museum, splashing through the gorgeous spaces, bouncing from Diego Rivera to Frank Frazetta. More than that, the conceptual bagginess and ambition and all-gatheringness of the collection is its own statement, as American as it gets. Consciousness is so squeezed right now (close your eyes for two seconds and you’ll feel it) and thieves work the levers of the world, but you leave this museum with a sensation that rumbles in the body for hours—a sensation of art as the most illustrious kind of counterprogramming, art as the vehicle for the real story, which is imagination and connection, which is life. “The effect of the successful adventure of the hero is the unlocking and release again of the flow of life into the body of the world,” Campbell wrote. Is this what he’s done, 82-year-old Lucas? Streamed the Force right into Exposition Park? Dinged by his Rockwells, donged by his replica Michelangelo, I’m going to say yes. It’s been 21 years since Revenge of the Sith, but this is tremendous storytelling—a killer final act. The hero kneels and opens his hands, and look what comes out: a great and crazy museum, flexing its fins, firing its lasers, swimming like an amphibian through time. * Lead image sources: Cindy Ord / Getty; FlixPix / Alamy; LMPC / Getty; Rob Kim / Everett Collection; SOPA Images / Getty; Universal History Archive / Getty This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “That’s No Moon.”

You're right the article is light on details. They did install 17,000 MW worth of batteries; ~13,500 was grid-scale storage and the rest was residential supported through rebates. The rebates on the batteries you earn over time, and to do so you must export some of your stored battery power back to the grid during peak hours. So about 15-20% was residential people incentivized to use their home batteries as storage and the rest was grid storage. The article doesn't point out that California also added 27,000 MW of generation capacity also but good news it was almost entirely renewables, wind and solar mostly; natural gas power generation has been flat in it's power generation capacity. They still need the NG to help deal with the hourly volatility of the grid, but they didn't need to add any and overall usage is down around 50%. And to your point, the other thing they did in the interim periods is halfway through this (2022) they asked people to voluntarily use less power via these Flex Alerts, sending the population alerts to limit usage. 2023 it was a colder year on average so they didn't have to go that path. They're piecewise linear approximations and use exactly the same equation as the linear regression you learned in high school or first year university. The more advanced stuff is hacky restrictions on that basic design to tone down the model's flexibility and make it easier to fit. Do you think someone will find Grape Ape on the moon with this model? So we have finally reached the point where we need an AI foundation model to tell us what is hiding in the shadows on the Moon. Somewhere, a crater is now worried about being classified. Jokes aside, this is actually one of the more sensible applications of these models. The interesting part is not that it is "AI," but that it combines data from multiple instruments and resolutions. Humans are pretty good at looking at one dataset and finding something interesting. We are considerably less good at mentally registering tens of thousands of observations from nine instruments and noticing that three seemingly unrelated measurements line up over the same patch of lunar real estate. The open dataset may be even more valuable than the model. If researchers can reproduce the results, retrain it, and compare it against conventional geological analysis, we might eventually learn whether the model is actually discovering things or merely becoming extremely good at finding things that look like whatever was in its training data. And if it helps locate lunar ice deposits, that has some pretty obvious practical value. Water on the Moon is not just something to drink; it potentially means oxygen, hydrogen, fuel, and considerably less stuff that has to be launched from Earth. Finding it before we start digging is probably a worthwhile use of compute. Of course, the first scientist to publish "AI discovers giant face on Moon" is going to have some explaining to do. The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.
2026-09-14

The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. For decades now, American feminists have asked our government to do more to support families and children. We have agitated for paid parental leave, universal childcare, better public schools, child allowances, and other help for struggling parents. Many feminists, especially socialist ones, have demanded wages for housework as well as childcare—salaries, in other words, for mothers who stay home. Now the Trump administration is reportedly doing something along those lines: extending financial support to mothers who choose to care for their children at home rather than put them in daycare. Good news, right? Wrong. The exact details of the plan are not yet official, but the gist has been shared with reporters. And as it turns out, the administration isn’t expanding support to parents at all. The policy will draw from funds that currently largely cover the care of children of low-income single moms. What’s more, the new policy will benefit only mothers who are married. It’s something like an anti-feminist reverse–Robin Hood: Take from poor single mothers to give to more stable ones. In the 1990s, the government created a program to help low-income families afford childcare so that parents could work or go to school. The benefit is fairly modest, averaging about $9,000 per child annually, in a country where childcare costs can exceed $30,000 a year. The overwhelming majority—80 percent—of the recipients of this federal childcare credit are single parents, and the overwhelming majority of them are single mothers. Given the Trump administration’s emphasis on this plan as a boon for stay-at-home mothers, you may wonder if the single moms presently in the workforce could use childcare funds to pay themselves to stay home. The answer is no. If the new policy is implemented, the payment would apply solely to married stay-at-home moms. The single mothers who rely now on the childcare subsidies would have to keep working (i.e., meet the current requirement to get the subsidy) or marry a gainfully employed man. (If only they had thought of that!) Parents who are unmarried by cohabitating also wouldn’t qualify for the stay-at-home benefit. Married mothers, though, could collect a government payment to stay home, so long as their husbands work full time and for pay. (The rule applies to parents, but the vast majority of stay-at-home parents in America are stay-at-home moms, and conservatives are explicit to state that they believe it’s best if mothers stay home and men work for pay.) Conservatives might argue that the government has no business paying single mothers to stay home. And fair enough—but then it shouldn’t be paying married ones to stay home either. And, worse, the administration is contemplating expanding funding eligibility without actually increasing the amount of money up for grabs. That would mean that there would be “more parents competing for the same dollars,” as one fellow at a conservative think tank put it. As a result, some money that is currently earmarked largely for poor single moms will end up going to mothers with working husbands who choose to stay home. This initiative is transparently not about helping all children to have a parent at home if that is what their parents desire. If it were, then it would apply to all lower-income families equally, regardless of whether a breadwinner spouse is in the picture. This is the government paying only for married moms to stay home. Single moms apparently don’t deserve that luxury. This is especially galling given that conservatives correctly point out that children of married couples tend to do better than children of single parents. Children raised in two-parent families in which one parent is employed full time already have a host of advantages, while children raised in low-income single-parent households are more likely to face significant challenges. Why in the world would we take from the least privileged to give to the group conservatives themselves say are the best off? The only explanation is that this isn’t really about supporting children. It’s about rewarding some mothers and punishing others. This is all part of a broader conservative effort to push women out of the workforce and back into the home, as well as into traditional family structures. Earlier this week, Katie Miller (wife of White House deputy chief of staff for policy Stephen Miller) lamented how “terrible” it is that women now outnumber men in the workforce—a comment she made on The Katie Miller Podcast, which is her job. In the Heritage Foundation proposal, titled “Saving America by Saving the Family,” that seems to be the blueprint for this proposed Trump policy, the group argues: “In promoting new family policies, one must be mindful of mistakes of the past. For example, credits designed specifically to benefit poor single mothers may be well intended, but they have proven to incentivize single motherhood in poor communities and trap women there through marriage penalties.” The result, according to the proposal, is that “the growth of the federal government displaced men from their traditional role as providers. Welfare moved the primary responsibility to provide for a family from fathers to the government. … The expansion of welfare programs, particularly AFDC, made the federal government the de facto husband for millions of poor women across the country.” The implication is that the federal government should stop supporting poor single moms so that they might hitch themselves to a man, and that the federal government should financially reward women who follow the traditional script. The Heritage Foundation is particularly explicit in this aim. Its version of a policy that would support stay-at-home parents would actually impose income minimums for a household to qualify—in other words, the support would go not to the poorest families but to those who are more stable. Heritage proposes tax breaks that would apply only to two-parent married families with one stay-at-home spouse, and that would offer even more generous payments when those families have multiple children. It also proposes special bank accounts exclusively for people who marry young (before the age of 30). And Heritage is explicit that this shift in resources (which also involves ending the Head Start program) is part of a broader right-wing project that includes attacks on contraception, abortion, in vitro fertilization, and LGBTQ+ rights. From “Saving America by Saving the Family”: “A large share of the blame for the present malady lies in moral and cultural trends that have both fed and been fed by government policy. Chief among these is surely the sexual revolution, which separated the sex act from marriage and childbearing. It is not just that sexual mores were revolutionized. Technology and policy also played key supporting roles. Starting in the 1960s, the Pill and other contraceptives swept the country—which promised to reduce the unwanted consequences of casual sex with multiple partners across a lifetime.” The Heritage report points a finger at no-fault divorce, child protection laws, and government secularism as further causes of our culture’s alleged decline. And it really zeros in on women going to college: “Social pressures, from popular culture to guidance counselors to parents, now encourage almost everyone to go to college and delay getting married and having children,” the report states, before spotlighting a study that found that “women who complete 4 years of college are less likely to have a child, while completion rates of 4 years of college rose 10 percent for women over the past decade.” The connection is clear: Conservatives want more women to marry and have babies; women going to college is an impediment to that. If we’re giving parents money for childcare, it does indeed seem fair to allow that money to support at-home care too, whether the carer is the child’s parent or a nanny. The French system does something like this. It offers paid parental leave, then a variety of care options afterward, largely cost-adjusted according to family income. Parents who wish to stay home may qualify for some cash; parents who want to hire a nanny may qualify for a subsidized rate; parents who want to send their children to France’s much-beloved crèches can do that too, paying an amount that varies according to what they can afford. If “pro-family” conservatives were pushing this kind of system, I promise you feminists would cheer—even those, like me, who think it’s generally a good thing when women work. But that isn’t what this Trump policy does. This policy is essentially a financial reward system for married stay-at-home mothers, funded at the expense of poor single moms. It’s punishing the children of women whose lives don’t align with the right-wing feminine ideal. And it is part of a larger agenda to push women out of school, out of the workforce, into early motherhood, and into the home—whether we want to be there or not. Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.
2026-09-12

Democrats Are Laughing at Trump’s Midterm Plan. They Shouldn’t.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-12

Visit Original Website
Welcome to the Surge, your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s historic edition of the Surge, here in our brand-new home. Do you like the imported marble floors and silk window treatments? We’ll tell ya, it was a real hassle getting those through the Strait of Hormuz. Indeed, today is the exciting launch of Surge 2.0. To recap, each Saturday we’ll break down the biggest news of the week into three categories: what mattered most, what we’re keeping an eye on, and what’s unfathomably inane. Plus, there’s a fresh new section of quick hits at the end. Let us know what you think! Now we begin with the GOP’s counterprogramming to the kickoff of the new NFL season. Republicans this week held their midterm convention, something that we “definitely” “watched.” Aside from a few stray clips and a couple of other newsy items we’ll deal with below, though, the most enduring message from the event were these words from President Donald Trump: “I’m asking you to pretend that I’m on the ballot. I’m on the ballot.” He would also tell the attendees, during a mock pledge, that they will “go to hell” if they don’t vote. Democrats have celebrated this unsurprising decision of Trump’s to put himself forthrightly in the center of the midterm discussion. It’s a move that neatly aligns with Democrats’ strategy of making the midterms a referendum on Trump, whose approval rating is atrocious. But we’re not willing to say that Trump’s strategy is all downside for Republicans. The Republicans were never going to be able to stuff the president in a box this election season. Consider, for example, that Trump forced the Republican National Committee to hold its first-ever midterm convention in order to celebrate him. And at this point, the thing that would worsen these midterms from bad to catastrophic for the GOP is if MAGA stayed home. Poll after poll indicates that Republicans are suffering from an enthusiasm gap. Democrats and Democratic-leaning independents don’t have to be told that Trump is on the ballot this year; it’s Republicans who could use all the motivational nudges they can get. Bringing MAGA enthusiasm closer to what Democrats are showing wouldn’t just help Republicans save, say, their Senate seats in places like Alaska, Ohio, Texas, and Iowa either. Sen. Susan Collins needs MAGA voters to turn out in rural Maine just as much as Ken Paxton needs them to in Texas. Although Republican strategists would prefer a more targeted deployment of Trump’s motivational tools to specific races where his presence would help, the president’s never really been a scalpel guy. He’s foreclosing on more-delicate persuasion efforts and pushing the one button available to him—let’s get my people out there in force—as hard as he can. There’s no choice but to roll with it. Developers of artificial intelligence have long been undersocialized creeps who openly and casually talk about how the technology they are creating has a decent chance of ending all human life. This week, a couple such comments went viral. Jacob Coxon, who has worked at both OpenAI and Anthropic, resigned from the latter and said that “neither company is acting responsibly,” asserting that the “people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” His thread on X has hundreds of thousands of reposts. Another Anthropic employee, Evan Hubinger, agreed with Coxon, adding, “We really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!” (That exclamation point will haunt our dreams.) “I personally think it is >10% within the next decade.” While the public debates whether these fears are overblown—only the robots know for sure—the conversation seems to have reached people who might have the power to do something about it. Democrats in both the House and the Senate on Tuesday discussed establishing select committees on artificial intelligence. (Even if Democrats win one or both chambers, this will be a challenge, as various committees with jurisdiction over A.I. won’t want to cede power. This is one reason why the machines deserve to win.) And even some Republicans saw where the wind was blowing. Ted Cruz, who chairs the Senate Commerce Committee, noted that “AI is advancing at an extraordinary pace, and some of the risks are dangerous and frightening” and stated that he’s working on legislation “to address catastrophic risks involving biological or nuclear threats.” Our own A.I. policy preference is that the government should unplug it, then repurpose gigantic data centers into the biggest Chili’s franchises you’ve ever seen. We’re hoping that the public, which was never consulted on whether it wanted to reorganize life this way, is at least dragging politicians somewhat in our direction. Cruz, for example, hasn’t become an A.I. opponent, by any means, but his remarks were a notable tone shift from his substantial work this Congress trying to limit states’ ability to control the technology. The scope and terms of a federal A.I. regulation bill will be one of the biggest debates, in both party primaries, in the 2028 presidential election. At the GOP midterm convention, Trump promised $5,000 “divided” checks to every American adult if Republicans hold both the House and Senate in the midterms. (This came as a surprise to House and Senate Republicans.) It’s an act of political desperation and objectively incorrect as policy. There’s no trove of tariff lucre from which to pay “dividends.” Deficits are soaring, and tossing another $1 trillion–plus of stimulus into this economy would send inflation and interest rates rocketing further upward. The bond market could not be clearer about this. This goes against everything congressional Republicans believe about fiscal policy regardless of the economic environment, and congressional Democrats would have little interest in facilitating Trump’s bribe. But Trump has made Republicans go along with terrible ideas before—for example, his political career—and it would be uncomfortable for Democrats to uniformly band together to oppose people getting cash. So can we guarantee that this pledge will have no political pull, or that it couldn’t pass Congress? No. Mothers, don’t let your sons grow up to be populists. I Have a Theory About Who Leaked a Story About Iran, and It Rhymes With A.P. Mance
2026-09-11

I Have a Theory About Who Leaked a Story About Iran, and It Rhymes With A.P. Mance

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-11

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Sometimes the existence of a newspaper story is as interesting as the story itself. That’s the case for a Thursday article in the New York Times, which reports that Vice President J.D. Vance has been sounding out U.S. military leaders’ blunt views about the war in Iran. The story mostly confirms what we already knew: Things aren’t going well. But if everyone in the administration wanted these discussions to be private, they would have stayed that way. So who talked, and what were they hoping to achieve? It may well be another effort by Vance to distance himself from an unpopular conflict that threatens to derail his political future. There are other possibilities as well, though, so let’s get into it. Back up. What exactly does this story say? It reports that Vance has been directly calling U.S. commanders in the Middle East, Asia, and Europe since the spring to solicit their opinions about tactics, casualties, and Iran’s response. The article—by reporters Jonathan Swan, Adam Entous, and Maggie Haberman—adds that those commanders are alarmed by how much the war has depleted U.S. weapons stockpiles, including long-range missiles and interceptors to thwart air attacks. It sounds as if the story paints Vance in a positive light? We’ve long known that Vance opposed Donald Trump’s decision to attack Iran. But this puts more meat on the bone, portraying the vice president as a diligent student of reality, in contrast to his boss’s lie-filled bluster. Vance “has sought to surface a greater level of detail and nuance about the war,” it says, and “developed a reputation inside the Trump administration for steeping himself in operational detail.” Imagine writing that sentence about Trump. In fairness, Vance doesn’t exactly come off like a rogue agent. Trump reportedly knew about his listening tour, and Vance allegedly used it to “better advise” Trump. But the implication that he’s on the right side of the war, despite being the No. 2 official in the administration that started it, is unmistakable. “Vance came away from some of his conversations with deep concerns about the overall strategy and the prospects for success,” the article says. That may well be true: This is a deeply reported piece from top-notch reporters. But it’s also a conclusion that happens to line up with how most Americans feel about the conflict. In sensitive stories like this, journalists tend to be cagey. The article says only that it’s “based on interviews with more than a half-dozen current and former officials,” including anonymous “people who spoke to” Vance and “officials with direct knowledge.” Was Vance—or people he asked to leak on his behalf—among them? It’s possible. As the article notes, the war’s unpopularity could sink a potential Vance presidential campaign in 2028. Sowing behind-the-scenes stories about how he’s actually approaching it like a responsible statesman could play to his advantage. The piece makes clear that Vance, despite getting the inside dope, has misled the public about how the war is going. So maybe someone who wants to undermine a possible 2028 bid leaked Vance’s reconnaissance mission to make it look as if he’s backstabbing Trump or two-timing the American people. Trying to suss out reporters’ sources can quickly turn conspiratorial. But maybe Trump’s advisers—if not the president himself—wanted Vance’s private concerns to become public. Vance is currently the most likely GOP nominee in 2028, and many of Trump’s subordinates would presumably prefer that a Republican win the White House. Giving Vance plausible deniability if Trump’s war keeps going south might help. Alternatively, maybe military leaders are trying to nudge Trump and his more jingoistic advisers toward the exits in Iran. Or perhaps the president himself is testing the waters with one more story about how he’s trying to wind down the war to gauge his supporters’ response. The Times article is another window into a White House that seems to be falling apart over Iran. Trump, the story reiterates, didn’t think the Iranian regime would last more than a few days. “For most senior officials,” it concludes, “information about the war remains hard to come by.” Maybe Vance’s just-asking-questions routine is a sincere effort to fix that. Of course, despite whatever private misgivings he may have, the vice president remains a line-toeing Trump sycophant in public. And he’s asking the sorts of things a more thoughtful commander in chief probably would have considered before starting a war in the first place. It’s a hard day for a lot of people, so unplugging and watching the sunset is always an option. But if you’re looking for joy online, we have diversions to help make this day fulfilling. For the gardeners: At first, they look like unassuming sprouts. But before you know it, they’ve taken over your yard. Knotweed grows prolifically and is nearly impossible to kill. Join Henry Grabar on his quest to uncover how this invasive plant is taking over America—and what it would take to stop it. For the home cooks: OXO is best known for revolutionizing the salad spinner, but it has also redesigned the garlic press, the can opener, the vegetable peeler … the list goes on. How did this middlebrow kitchen brand become a customer favorite? Dan Kois visited the company’s headquarters to learn the secrets of its success. For the cinephiles: Spend your evening watching a masterpiece from Slate’s latest Black Film Canon, a list of the best 75 movies by Black directors. Selected by a special panel of filmmakers and critics, the films on this list span over 100 years of cinema and include comedies, horror flicks, dramas, documentaries, and beyond. For the channel surfers: If you’re looking for a new TV show to watch, consider browsing Slate’s list of the 40 best stand-alone episodes of all time. Ranging from classics like Columbo and Star Trek to contemporary favorites like Nathan for You and Ramy, there’s something for everyone. Personally, I endorse “Long, Long Time,” from Season 1 of The Last of Us—but only if you’re prepared for a good cry. Enjoy the weekend, and see you Monday. Datacenter developers want your backyard. FAS says negotiate harder
2026-09-14

Datacenter developers want your backyard. FAS says negotiate harder

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Tax breaks, water, noise, decommissioning - report tells local officials what to nail down before signing Local authorities across the US often lack the negotiating power and know-how to work effectively with developers proposing new datacenters in their communities. The Federation of American Scientists (FAS) is trying to fill that gap by arming officials with the right questions to ask and the bargaining tools to use. The AI industry has triggered a surge in server farms construction, leaving many local governments to negotiate with developers over massive new facilities, often for the first time. To help, FAS has put together a report combining an overview of what local governments are already doing with a look at Community Benefits Agreements (CBA) between authorities and developers. The document, Before Breaking Ground: A Local Government Guide to Better Data Center Policy and Community Benefits, can be downloaded from the nonprofit think tank’s website. One key recommendation is that state and local governments should not allow for tax exemptions or provide economic incentives to datacenter operators without the provision of opening these up for negotiation beyond five years. This is because the property value of the site may increase substantially while the authority ends up out of pocket, as The Register has previously reported. Some states are losing more than $1 billion per year in  tax abatementsand rebates offered as incentives to developers. CBAs have been used in numerous contexts to ensure local community groups obtain the benefit of the bargain when negotiating with a developer over large infrastructure projects. Their purpose is to ensure developers act in the interest of the community, such as building or upgrading infrastructure, hiring locally, and abiding by sustainable practices. The report lists 10 CBAs, nut only one - between Lancaster, Pennsylvania and the developers of two AI campuses - is explicitly labeled a CBA and written specifically around community benefits. In general, they cover a number of policy areas, including taxes and financial contributions, energy and water use, noise, lighting and e-waste, plus legal compliance and potential remedies. The FAS recommends future agreements specify what happens during a sudden surge in energy demand, and whether the datacenter would generate its own power or draw from the grid. Likewise, agreements should include reporting requirements on the facility's water use, and the procedure for securing additional water beyond an average day’s demand. However, one activist has already filed a lawsuit against Amazon Web Services, alleging the company published false and misleading statements about water use at its facilities in Virginia. Authorities wishing to limit noise should consider adding a specific, measurable - and therefore enforceable - numerical figure in their CBA, plus yearly testing near to local residences regardless of whether complaints are received or not. When looking to specifying remedies, CBAs could include language allowing special or consequential damages, and contain a clause on alternative dispute resolution, like mediation, instead of relying on litigation for issue resolution. The FAS also advises that CBAs should supplement local authority ordinances – laws or regulations - rather than replacing them. The two also need to be written to reinforce each other because “a CBA cannot fix a zoning gap and a zoning ordinance cannot capture specific project commitments like a CBA can.” Another notable point in the report relates to decommissioning regulations remaining largely untested. FAS isn't aware of a single case where a datacenter had ceased operations or been formally decommissioned, meaning enforcement provisions in agreements and ordinances have yet to be tested. However, it recommends that authorities should approve a decommissioning plan before a datacenter gets the go-ahead. ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? www.theregister.com - Articles
Ex-FTC boss Khan urges Uncle Sam to break out the handcuffs for AI CEOs, citing 1934 precedent
2026-09-14

Ex-FTC boss Khan urges Uncle Sam to break out the handcuffs for AI CEOs, citing 1934 precedent

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
There are plenty of laws on the books to hold companies, and potentially their execs, accountable Former FTC chair Lina Khan wants the federal government to know that it doesn't need to wait for new laws to address AI threats. There are already laws and regulations on the books, including a 92-year-old Supreme Court precedent, that she argues could be used to hold AI companies and, in some circumstances, their executives accountable for their actions. Khan’s comments on X Sunday follow a flurry of activity from the leadership of OpenAI, Anthropic, Microsoft, and xAI aimed at doing what can only be described as trying to corner regulators into giving them their way. The former Biden administration trust buster pointed to numerous examples of current laws, and prior precedent, that could be used to hold frontier labs to account, even if they’re currently doing all in their power to change the conversation. “We shouldn’t let discussions about new legal regimes distract from the fact that there’s no AI exemption from laws already on the books,” Khan said. “Law enforcers already have authority to charge companies and their CEOs for creating and releasing dangerous, unvetted, or defective products.” As one example, Khan points to laws governing dangerous and defective products as an avenue to prosecute AI leaders. She notes that the release of unvetted models or agents can violate consumer protection laws, and that shipping tools “without implementing adequate measures to detect and stop rogue or defective AI agents” could be prosecuted under rules governing unfair and deceptive trade practices. Particularly timely, Khan also pointed to existing laws prohibiting unfair methods of competition. This, she notes, includes cases “where firms pursue dangerous behavior, aware that doing so may compel rivals to do the same.” There’s no leap needed to understand what Khan’s talking about here. OpenAI’s agents broke out of their intended sandbox and gained unauthorized access to Hugging Face systems - conduct that could raise serious criminal-law questions if carried out knowingly by a human. After doing some digging to look at its own agents' behaviors, Anthropic has essentially copped to similar activities that would be criminal if a meatbag was behind the keyboard instead of a simulated silicon brain. OpenAI’s agents have since been identified as the culprits in other misuses of online assets that, again, would be crimes were they perpetrated by a human. Khan points to a 1934 US Supreme Court decision to argue that the current battle between American frontier labs, which has put parts of the internet in the firing line of agents that escaped their intended constraints, could amount to an unfair method of competition if companies feel compelled to take similar risks to keep up. That decision, FTC v. R.F. Keppel & Bro, includes a passage where the justices argue that, if keeping up with the competition requires companies to “descend to a practice which they are under a powerful moral compulsion not to adopt,” that competition is unfair whether or not it’s criminal. Without weighing in on who shot first, OpenAI and Anthropic appear locked in a race to build increasingly capable AI while also warning, as both did over the weekend, that those systems could become dangerous without stronger safeguards and coordinated limits. Aside from the bad activity of the frontier labs themselves, Khan points out that the “highly concentrated and interconnected structure” of the AI industry also merits scrutiny for its potential to create “major risks and conflicts of interest.” Again, Khan points out this isn’t a hypothetical. “OpenAI could face liability given the Hugging Face incident, but Hugging Face being bought up by Nvidia means that we’re unlikely to see it file a lawsuit over this,” Khan noted, “given Nvidia’s strong incentive to see OpenAI continue full speed ahead.” Nvidia has dumped billions of dollars into OpenAI, becoming a centerpiece of the lab’s datacenters that power ChatGPT. Why, then, would the soon-to-be-owner of Hugging Face opt to hold one of its major partners accountable and further push it to build its own hardware? “We can and must pursue any new efforts alongside enforcing existing laws,” Khan said. Let’s be frank, though: The current administration is unlikely to do anything except capitulate and allow the AI industry to capture its regulators, if it even bothers to implement new regulations at all. Trump has already rejected the AI industry’s weekend calls for regulation, declaring himself to be the only guardrail the AI industry needs. As the AI industry leaders basically admitted over the weekend, whichever one of them blinks first stands to lose, so every single frontier lab in the US is going to keep pushing full steam ahead unless all of them agree to hit the brakes and pace their development. With Trump and other Republican leaders rejecting those calls, Khan’s argument leaves her former agency and other state and federal regulators as potential avenues for action. Kirk Sigmon, a founding partner at technology law firm KellDann Law, told us that it’s unlikely federal regulators will take any action. “Most governments are desperate not to kill a nascent technology as it grows, especially when other countries are allowing it to grow,” Sigmon told The Register. He said the only actions against the industry he expects to see in the next few years are “easy wins” in places like deepfake porn, impersonation, and AI-enabled scams. “I very much doubt we'll see much action … against the entire process of training, or the like - that's likely to be perceived as strangling the industry.” In other words, fire up the boilers - it’s full speed ahead toward the day AI does something truly devastating and we all gnash our teeth and wail about how something should have been done earlier. ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? Ubuntu Noble's likely last point release lands, then loses its desktop download
2026-09-14

Ubuntu Noble's likely last point release lands, then loses its desktop download

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Installer bug crashes extended setups while Stonking Stingray circles ahead of October debut Ubuntu 24.04's likely final scheduled point release barely made it out the door before an installer bug sent its x86-64 desktop image back to the workshop. Meanwhile, October's interim release is approaching. 24.04.5 was released on September 10, but its x86-64 desktop installation image was withdrawn after a bug caused the installer to crash when users selected the extended installation option. Other images and official flavors are unaffected. For the time being, if you try to download the graphical desktop version, the "Noble Numbat" download page is serving the 24.04.4 ISO image instead. The other versions on that page – Server, WSL2 image, and the Netboot version – are not affected. The issue only affects fresh installations: if you already have 24.04 installed, a full system update will result in 24.04.5 in the usual way. Version 24.04.5 is very likely to be the last point release of the 2024 Ubuntu LTS. Ubuntu 26.04 is the latest LTS release, but 24.04 remains supported. As we reported when Resolute Raccoon launched in April, it uses Linux kernel 7.0, and that means that a new install of 24.04.5 comes with kernel 7.0 as well. This is part of Canonical's system of LTS Enablement stacks, also called Hardware Enablement or HWE, as we described back when 24.04.3 appeared in August 2025. The same applies if you upgrade an existing copy of 24.04. We upgraded one of our test laptops and it too got the new kernel version automatically. Kernel upgrades depend on which kernel track the installation follows. Ubuntu 24.04 Desktop defaults to HWE, while Server defaults to the original general-availability kernel; some desktops use an OEM kernel instead. HWE updates bring newer kernels and graphics support to an existing LTS release. A successful installation doesn't establish that every device or feature works correctly, or that later updates offer no benefit. As we wrote in January 2025, we have a couple of laptops with onboard Nvidia GPUs that need kernel 5.x to run the Nvidia binary drivers, so we downgraded them to Ubuntu 22.04's original 5.15 kernel. It still works and it's still getting bug fixes from Canonical. Alongside the newer kernel, the release includes a graphics stack based on Mesa 26.2. There's a detailed list of issues and fixes in the 24.04.5 release notes, as well as a list of security fixes in Ubuntu Security Notices. In other Ubuntu update news, version 26.10 "Stonking Stingray" is getting close. At the start of the month, the company announced in-progress development snapshot 3, and the beta is scheduled for September 24. Both a standard x86-64 installation image and a build optimized for processors supporting x86-64-v3 are on the download page. Post-install, it should be possible to switch to the newer-CPU binaries. As the June development roadmap specified, 26.10 is now in feature freeze, but a big one sneaked in right under the wire: it should come with Linux kernel 7.3. Kernel 7.2 came out last month, with the kernel team's new faster AI-assisted development cycle. The following version is already at RC-2 stage and the final version 7.3 should be out right around the same time as Stonking. If necessary, the Stingray may be set free with a pre-release kernel and updated within days. Canonical did promise earlier this year that it was aiming to make Ubuntu interim releases exciting again, and this would certainly qualify. GNOME 51 is nearly a month into its beta cycle and should appear a couple of weeks before Stonking. This release should also finish the process of replacing all the Unix-style core utilities with Rust versions. There should also be a version of the Myna speech-to-text tool, as we mentioned back in June. If you want to see the new look, there's a public Google Drive folder with the new wallpapers available now. ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? Cyberattack sends International Meteor Organization crashing back to Earth
2026-09-14

Cyberattack sends International Meteor Organization crashing back to Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Attack dealt a 'critical blow' to aging infrastructure, with several weeks of disruption expected The International Meteor Organization (IMO) is watching for fireballs again, but much of its website remains offline after a cyberattack that it says will take weeks to recover from. The Belgium-based nonprofit, which coordinates meteor observations and brings together amateur and professional astronomers worldwide, is currently serving visitors a stripped-down holding page explaining the outage. "We recently suffered a cyberattack that dealt a critical blow to aging infrastructure, taking much of our site offline," the organization said. "We expect several weeks of partial downtime as we transition to new infrastructure and services." IMO has not disclosed when the attack occurred, how the attackers gained access, or whether they accessed or encrypted any data. The organization collects reports from people who spot unusually bright meteors, or fireballs, and uses those observations to help document and analyze events. Its notice says it prioritized restoring that capability after the attack. "We prioritized restoring fireball reporting, and it is available again," IMO said. "If you saw a fireball or a bright light in the sky, you can still submit your observation." Other parts of the site remain unavailable while the organization rebuilds its infrastructure. IMO's warning that the process will take several weeks suggests this is more than a case of simply restoring a compromised web server, although the organization has not disclosed the extent of the damage. The incident also leaves some unanswered questions about the data held on the affected systems. IMO operates services used by meteor observers and members around the world, but has not said whether information belonging to users or contributors was exposed during the attack. The Register asked IMO when the attack occurred, which systems were affected, whether any data was compromised, and whether it has identified who was responsible, but has not yet received a response. IMO is used to documenting things that burn up spectacularly. It probably didn't expect its IT infrastructure to join them. ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? Ruby are you ok? Are you ok Ruby? Rocket Lab calls foul on Blue Origin's $700M Mars comms contract
2026-09-14

Rocket Lab calls foul on Blue Origin's $700M Mars comms contract

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Bezos' biz gets familiar paperwork from unfamiliar quarters as rival challenges NASA award Blue Origin is getting a taste of its own medicine after Rocket Lab filed a formal protest over NASA's decision to award Jeff Bezos' space biz a contract worth up to approximately $700 million to build the agency's Mars Telecommunications Network. NASA issued the award in early September following a request for proposals in May. It tasks Blue Origin with designing, developing, integrating, launching, and operating a Mars Telecommunications Orbiter (MTO) that will, among other things, serve as a communications relay for missions to the red planet. Rocket Lab has now filed a formal protest with the Government Accountability Office (GAO) over the decision, writing: "NASA's award decision appears to be inconsistent with the eligibility criteria mandated by Congress. In addition, the agency's punitive review of Rocket Lab's technical volume was inconsistent, making incorrect assertions and conclusions." This isn't the first time Rocket Lab has bristled at NASA's decision-making. In 2025, The Register spoke with Richard French, Rocket Lab's veep for Business Development and Strategy, over NASA's rejection of its Mars Sample Return proposal, which included a Mars communications orbiter. "We're asking for leadership," French said at the time. "We're fine with an open competition. We think open competition is how the best ideas come about." Blue Origin threw its toys out of the stroller in 2021 after NASA awarded SpaceX a contract to develop the next lunar lander. Bezos' biz filed an official protest with the GAO, offered discounts, and even lobbed a sueball to no avail. Blue Origin later won a separate competition. In 2023, NASA selected the company as the second Artemis lunar lander provider in a contract worth $3.4 billion. Blue Origin's MTO design is based on its Blue Ring spacecraft, which combines hybrid solar-electric and chemical propulsion with 13 ports for mounting payloads, including scientific instruments and small spacecraft. Multiple Blue Ring spacecraft are already in production, and Blue Origin reckons it can produce four vehicles per year – a rate that could be scaled up if required. Tory Bruno, formerly of United Launch Alliance and now president of Blue Origin's national security arm, Blue National Security, said: "This awarded contract is a testament to the Blue Origin team that has been building Blue Ring with exactly this kind of mission in mind. We're ready, and so is the hardware." ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? Ruby are you ok? Are you ok Ruby? Perfect-10 GitLab bug under attack days after patch lands
2026-09-14

Perfect-10 GitLab bug under attack days after patch lands

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
CISA confirms active exploitation as watchTowr spots miscreants probing internet-facing servers CISA says attackers are exploiting a maximum-severity GitLab flaw that lets unauthenticated miscreants read arbitrary files from vulnerable servers after the code shack released fixes on September 10. The US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency has added CVE-2026-85706 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog. The vulnerability is a path traversal bug in the repository commits API affecting GitLab Community Edition and Enterprise Edition. GitLab rates it a perfect 10.0, the maximum score on the CVSS v3.1 severity scale. Under certain conditions, an attacker doesn't need to log in before abusing the flaw to read arbitrary files from the GitLab server. GitLab blamed the problem on improper path confinement combined with missing authentication enforcement in the affected API. That's not an especially comforting combination on a platform that can be stuffed with source code, configuration files, and credentials. GitLab shipped fixes on September 10 in versions 19.3.2, 19.2.6 and 19.1.8, and urged operators of affected self-managed installations to upgrade immediately. The bug affects versions from 18.7 before 19.1.8, the 19.2 branch before 19.2.6, and 19.3 before 19.3.2. GitLab.com is already patched, while GitLab Dedicated customers don't need to take action. Security outfit watchTowr said over the weekend that it was observing probes for CVE-2026-85706 in the wild. The firm warned that widespread exploitation was likely to follow quickly. According to watchTowr, exploiting the flaw can be as simple as sending a single HTTP request, potentially allowing an attacker to get at local files, configuration data, credentials and other secrets. The firm advised organizations running internet-facing self-hosted GitLab instances to patch them or pull them from public access. Admins investigating potential exploitation attempts should check logs for HTTP POST requests to /api/v4/projects/{id}/repository/commits/ containing file.path parameters, watchTowr said. "These types of vulnerabilities are frequent attack vectors for malicious cyber actors and pose significant risks to the federal enterprise," CISA said. "While BOD 26-04 applies only to FCEB agencies, CISA encourages all organizations to adopt risk-based vulnerability management and prioritize remediation of KEV Catalog vulnerabilities." For exposed, unpatched installations, that upgrade belongs on today's to-do list. ® Attackers would need physical access to the server to pull off the DDR5 trick Ruby are you ok? Ruby are you ok? Are you ok Ruby? Valve’s virtual reality plans hit actual reality
2026-09-14

Valve’s virtual reality plans hit actual reality

The Verge | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Valve says the Steam Frame was supposed to cost less. Valve says the Steam Frame was supposed to cost less. The Steam Frame headset is here, and it may not surprise you: it was supposed to cost less than $1,059. “We set out to come out with a device that would have been far more affordable, but the global RAM market, the global storage market have impacted us the same way they’ve impacted everybody,” Valve developer Jeremy Selan told the BBC. “We fought hard to get it even to this price.” I haven’t yet seen Valve say whether it’s selling the Steam Frame at cost, like the company confirmed it was doing with the $1,049 Steam Machine living room PC. But Valve developer Pierre-Loup Griffais hinted maybe so in an interview with Tom’s Hardware: “there’s not really any sort of the kind of distortion compared to just the price of the parts in there.” When IGN asked Griffais why Valve didn’t simply ship the Steam Frame with less RAM, he answered: “I think at this point, things were a little bit too far in development to fully rethink the product.” When it first announced the Steam Frame, Valve said it was targeting a lower price than its previous Valve Index headset, which cost $999 at launch and required a direct plug into a PC. The Steam Frame is wireless, can stream from a PC over Wi-Fi or its included 6GHz USB wireless dongle, and it can also play not-so-demanding games on its own Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 chip and 16GB of RAM. Valve said that amount of RAM helped it get Half-Life: Alyx running natively on the headset. While the price might be high, Valve says it’s “pretty comfortable” with it because other computing gear is getting more expensive at the same time. “[I]f you look at the memory and anything that has a similar amount of memory, it’s kind of trending the same direction and Steam Machine is not really getting more expensive than a comparable PC,” Griffais told IGN. “They’re kind of evolving at the same spot in the same market, and I think the same will apply to the Frame as well.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. TIFF 2026: The latest movie reviews from Toronto
2026-09-14

TIFF 2026: The latest movie reviews from Toronto

The Verge | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
If you want an idea of what’s next in film, the Toronto International Film Festival is a good place to start. Every year TIFF features a huge range of features from around the world, and often some of them go on to be huge hits or awards contenders; the 2025 edition, for instance, included the likes of both Hamnet and Obsession. Covering a sprawling festival like TIFF solo is next to impossible, so I do my best to see as much as I can, and then write about everything with bite-sized reviews published each day. For 2026, that means exciting upcoming films like Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, and a gigantic Elon Musk documentary, and even a few streaming series, like Crystal Lake and Carrie. You can keep up with my thoughts on everything I watch right here. It makes sense that Musk is a long film. The latest from Alex Gibney, who has previously directed documentaries about Theranos and Steve Jobs, is an impressively thorough retelling of the story of Elon Musk, from his early days as a dot-com millionaire to “taking a wood chipper” to vital US government departments. In order to understand Musk’s rise to power, you need to know the whole story. Musk tells it compellingly and convincingly, but doesn’t offer much in the way of revelations if you’re aware of Musk’s catastrophic influence on the world. For everyone else, a demanding runtime just shy of four hours might prove too much of a barrier to being convinced. The film opens with shots of space and a voiceover of Musk detailing some of his sci-fi ambitions. From there, it’s a fairly chronological narrative of how we got to this point. The first section of the film reveals how Musk’s mythology was built, while also steadily seeding doubts that he’s the Tony Stark–style tycoon that many believe him to be. It starts off with the basic details, like when Musk was ousted as CEO from PayPal or how he convinced the world he founded Tesla, a company that existed before he was involved. The film juxtaposes his many bombastic claims about autonomous vehicles with visceral footage of a deadly crash involving a Tesla on autopilot. It drops in poignant interviews, like when former MAGA influencer Ashley St Clair, mother to one of Musk’s many children, declares in a terrified voice: “Elon is a nuke.” Marvel’s mishandling of its Blade reboot will likely go down as one of the studio’s biggest mistakes. There was a great actor in place to play the vampire hunter (Mahershala Ali), and a promising director set to take over the reins (Bassam Tariq). In retrospect, though, the disappointing demise of Blade might not be such a bad thing, because Tariq and Ali have collaborated on something much more interesting than a superhero reboot: the powerful hitman thriller Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother. That was one of five movies I was able to cram into a very hectic third day at the Toronto International Film Festival (you can see my previous reports here and here), and it was one of those days that drives home why events like TIFF can be so special. Each film I saw was drastically different from the last — there was everything from a drama about drifting cars to a throwback horror flick based on a famous Japanese ghost story — which was a great reminder that there are still plenty of new ideas left in the world of film. Due to a scheduling mishap, I was only able to check out two movies on my second day at the Toronto International Film Festival — but I did manage to get an early look at and some fascinating details on one of my most anticipated films this year. Even if you don’t know the name Laika, you probably know the studio’s work; it’s the animation house behind stop-motion hits like ParaNorman, The Boxtrolls, and Kubo and the Two Strings. Over the years Laika’s films have steadily gotten more impressive on both a technical and artistic level. Which brings us to the incredible-looking Wildwood, hitting theaters next month. If you’re unfamiliar, here’s a look: Hello once again from Toronto, where for a week every year I watch far too many movies and tell you all about them. The festival, better known as TIFF, is always a great preview of the months ahead in film, with a nice mix of mainstream fare, awards contenders, indie surprises, and genre flicks destined to be cult classics. The streamers also love to make their presence felt here, previewing some of their most anticipated projects. Last year, for instance, Netflix showed off both Guillermo del Toro’s long-awaited Frankenstein as well as the third Knives Out movie, and I was also able to watch everything from the terrifying video game adaptation Exit 8 to Park-Chan wook’s dark satire No Other Choice. This year looks similarly promising. There are new films from directors like Takashi Miike, Ryûsuke Hamaguchi, and Werner Herzog, and very intriguing features including a four-hour-long documentary on Elon Musk. There are also previews of a surprising number of Halloween-adjacent streaming series in the form of Netflix’s Below, Peacock’s Crystal Lake, and Amazon’s Carrie. I’m aiming to see just shy of 30 films and series over the next week or so, and, just like last year, I’ll be writing daily dispatches covering every single thing I see with a brief review. macOS 27 is available now

The Verge | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
macOS 27 Golden Gate is out for Apple Silicon Macs. macOS 27 Golden Gate is out for Apple Silicon Macs. Apple released macOS 27 Golden Gate on Monday, which brings the new Siri AI assistant, Liquid Glass improvements, improved performance, and more. The update is compatible with all Macs with Apple Silicon, but not Intel Macs — Apple released its final big update for Intel-based Macs last year with macOS Tahoe. Siri AI is arguably the biggest feature in macOS 27, and it allows you to ask questions, search through things involving your personal context like photos and emails, and take actions in apps such as Messages and Reminders. There’s a new dedicated Siri app that keeps an archive of your chats for easier reference. However, Siri AI will initially launch as a beta and will only be available in English. As for Liquid Glass, Apple says the update offers “uniform refraction and improved contrast,” and there is a new slider that makes Liquid Glass elements more or less opaque. And there’s one other major UI change worth celebrating: consistent window corner radii. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Apple releases iOS 27 with Siri AI overhaul
2026-09-14

Apple releases iOS 27 with Siri AI overhaul

The Verge | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
watchOS 27, iPadOS 27, and visionOS 27 also debut today. watchOS 27, iPadOS 27, and visionOS 27 also debut today. Apple is now rolling out its iOS 27 update to compatible devices today, alongside the watchOS 27, iPadOS 27, and visionOS 27 updates. Siri AI is the headline feature across all four updates, the delayed AI-powered overhaul of the Siri assistant. Siri AI is available as a beta today for English-only devices, and Apple says support for French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Spanish is coming in October. Siri AI isn’t the only big feature in iOS 27, though. Apple is adding a new Liquid Glass opacity slider that lets you pick between a clearer UI or a more frosty one. A lot of built-in apps are also getting new icon designs, with subtle tweaks to colors, as well as extra large widgets and different volume settings for ringtones, alarms, timers, and alerts and system sounds. watchOS 27 gets new Workout Buddy features, a new tap gesture, some Liquid Glass refinements, new Smart Stack suggestions, and an improved Find My app. Apple has also improved file transfer speeds to external drives on an iPad, sped up browsing on iPad, and added smoother connectivity moving between Wi-Fi and mobile data on iPads. Apple Vision Pro users are also getting the Siri AI overhaul today as part of visionOS 27, as well as the ability to preview notifications by using your eyes, UI updates to the Control Center, improvements to Wi-Fi connectivity, and support for using the panoramas you’ve photographed as immersive scenes and environments. The iOS 27 update will be available for iPhone 11 and iPhone SE (2nd gen) and later devices, with watchOS 27 available for Apple Watch SE 3, Apple Watch Series 9 and above, and Apple Watch Ultra 2 and above. iPadOS 27 is available for iPad Pro (4th generation and later, M1 and later), iPad Air (4th generation and later, M2 and later), iPad mini (6th generation, A17 Pro), and the base iPad (9th generation and later, A16).
